/longitudinal data using some form of regression
[
VCF+18
]

[
FM14
]

[
MBH+14
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
GHG+18
]

[
MMV+08
]
. However, like the generalized Lotka-Volterra equations they also allow indefinite growth under some conditions, which as discussed in section
4.2
is unrealistic. Cf. also the discussion in appendices
A.1
and
A.2
.

8
“
t=0
” experiment

The “
t=0
” (“sub-”)experiment differs from the “full” experiment described in section
7
in that droplets are not incubated. Cells are not allowed to grow or divide. The droplets’ contents are sequenced immediately after formation, skipping the second phase from
4
. Section
8.1
reviews the relevant notation and observed data. Section
8.3
clarifies the statistical model. Section
8.4
discusses the original target parameter definitions. Section
8.5
explains why in practice one most likely has to use working models for this problem. Section
8.6
gives the default working model that an experimentalist would be expected to use, as well as corresponding projected target parameters. Section
8.7
outlines the reasons why we might expect this working model to be inadequate in practice. Section
8.8
defines a generalized working model which mitigates some of these problems, as well as corresponding projected target parameters.

8.1
Notation and Observed Data

Let “
{D}
” for “
d
roplets” denote the total number of microfluidic droplets formed from a sampling pool of cells, and

S

denote
the total number of cell types in the sampling pool. In the motivating microbial ecology example, “
S
” for “
s
trains” is the total number of microbial strains in the microbial community sample.

Given a positive integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
[N]:=\{1,\dots,n,\dots,N\}
. Unless specified otherwise, given a vector
\vec{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{S}
, its entries are denoted

\vec{\mathbf{v}}\overset{def}{=}(v^{(1)},\dots,v^{(s)},\dots,v^{(S)})\,.

(8.1)

The shorthand
{v:=\sum_{s=1}^{S}v^{(s)}}
is used for the sum of the entries of
\vec{\mathbf{v}}
.
Another useful notion is the
support
of a (non-negative) vector
\vec{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{S}
:

\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{v}}):=\{s\in[S]:v^{(s)}>0\}\subseteq[S]\,.

(8.2)

For a given droplet

d
(“
d
” for “
d
roplet”)
, the random variable

N_{d}(0)

is the number of cells from the population encapsulated in the droplet

d

during its formation (“
N
” for “
n
umber”). Equivalently,

N_{d}(0)

is the number of cells
in droplet
d

at “time
0
”. This is the sum of the entries of the random vector:

\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(0):=\left(N^{(1)}_{d}(0),\dots,N^{(s)}_{d}(0),\dots,N^{(S)}_{d}(0)\right)\,,

(8.3)

where for each type
s
(“
s
” for “
s
train”) of the
S
total types,

N^{(s)}_{d}(0)

is the random variable equalling the number of cells from type
s

in droplet
d

at time
0
.
These are the observed data.

For the sake of brevity, often the subscript
d
is omitted, i.e.
N(0)
,
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
, and
N^{(s)}(0)
are used instead. This highlights how the random variables corresponding to the droplets are assumed (for the sake of simplicity) to have
independent and identical probability distributions
, and thus how most statements should not depend on the specific droplet
d
.

For each cell type
s
, let
f^{(s)}
(“
f
” for “
f
requency”) denote the relative abundance of cell type
s
in the sampling pool. In particular,
\sum_{s=1}^{S}f^{(s)}=1
.

8.2
Statistical Motivation for Targeted Estimands

The treatment group
\mathcal{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
for the effect of strain
s_{1}
on strain
s_{2}
consists of droplets containing both strains. Likewise, the control group
\mathcal{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
for the effect of strain
s_{1}
on strain
s_{2}
consists of droplets containing strain
s_{2}
but not strain
s_{1}
. With these groups, we can quantify the effect of strain
s_{1}
on strain
s_{2}
by targeting an average treatment effect (ATE) or other similar causal estimand.

However, even when we can implement estimators for such causal estimands, sometimes their results may still not be scientifically reliable. For example, consider a situation where our treatment group
\mathcal{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
consists of only
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}=5
droplets and our control group
\mathcal{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
consists of
\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\approx\!50,000
droplets. This example may seem unrealistic, but it is actually optimistic compared to some of the size imbalances between the treatment and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
that one may expect to see in practice. Cf. section
2.C
. Regardless of the causal estimand we choose to quantify such interaction effects, in order for our estimates to be scientifically useful we need to estimate their reliability. We need to estimate their statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
.

Of course (even for a fixed causal estimand) the statistical power
\mathscr{P}
function will depend on the particular estimator, so at this level of generality we cannot complete such a calculation. However, a priori we do also know that the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
will always depend on the sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
,
\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
of the treatment group and control group, respectively. Thus, to estimate statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
, we always need to know the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
, regardless of the particular estimator.

Unfortunately, due to the nature of the experiment, the scientist can never directly control the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
. The treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
are random variables. Therefore, for any estimator of any causal estimand, the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
will itself be a random variable. This is not inherently a “deal-breaker”, but we do still need to constrain the possibilities for the distribution of the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
. At the very least, we would like to be able to compute the expected statistical power
\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})]
.

Specifically, for a given estimator of a given causal estimand, the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
will be a deterministic function
\mathscr{P}
of the treatment and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
(and possibly other factors which for simplicity we will assume to be known and fixed). If we know specific values
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}=T^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
,
\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}=C^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
for the treatment and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
, then we can evaluate this statistical power function
\mathscr{P}
and get a deterministic value
\mathscr{P}(T^{(s_{1},s_{2})},C^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
. If instead the treatment and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
are random variables, then when evaluating this statistical power function we get a new random variable
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
whose distribution is determined by the pushforward measure.

Obviously, without specifying the particular causal estimand and its particular estimator, we still cannot complete the calculation of the expectation
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})\right]
of a random statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
. Nevertheless, for any given estimator of any given causal estimand, the distribution of the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
will be determined by the distribution of the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
. Thus, for any estimator of any causal estimand, as a prerequisite for being able to estimate expected statistical power
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})\right]
, we must first understand the distribution of the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
.

Note that the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
can be understood as sums of indicator random variables:

\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}=\sum_{d\in[{D}]}\mathbb{I}\!\left\{d\text{'th droplet in }\mathcal{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right\}\,,\quad\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}=\sum_{d\in[{D}]}\mathbb{I}\!\left\{d\text{'th droplet in }\mathcal{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right\}\,.

(8.4)

To completely characterize the distribution of indicator random variables it suffices to know their expectations, i.e. the probabilities:

\mathbb{P}\left(d\text{'th droplet in }\mathcal{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right)\,,\quad\quad\mathbb{P}\left(d\text{'th droplet in }\mathcal{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right)\,.

(8.5)

If we assume the droplets are identically distributed, then linearity of expectation allows us to compute the expected treatment and control group sizes:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right]={D}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(d\text{'th droplet in }\mathcal{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right)\,,\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right]={D}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(d\text{'th droplet in }\mathcal{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right)\,.

(8.6)

This is already enough to compute
\mathscr{P}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}\right]\right)
, a quantity that we can use as a (potentially suboptimal
28
28

28

Cf. the “arithmetic” estimands from section
7.E
.

) stand-in for the expected statistical power
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})\right]
in situations where we don’t want to make additional assumptions about the distributions of the droplets.

However, if we additionally assume that the droplets are mutually independent, then we can compute the distributions of the treatment and control group sizes as marginals of a Multinomial distribution
29
29

29

Cf. sections
2.3
and
2.4
.
. Given the statistical power function
\mathscr{P}
, i.e. choice of a particular causal estimand and estimator thereof, this would then in principle
30
30

30

In practice, given realistic problem sizes, evaluating this exact formula would most likely be intractable, such that one would probably also need to use additional heuristics and approximations. The choice of those heuristics and approximations would depend on the particular causal estimand and estimator thereof, and thus is outside the scope of this discussion.

be enough information to compute the distribution of the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
or any summary statistic thereof.

One might object to treating the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
as a random variable. Certainly, after the experiment has been run, the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
will be known, allowing us to condition on their observed values
T^{(s_{1},s_{2})},C^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
and “restore determinism” by evaluating
\mathscr{P}(T^{(s_{1},s_{2})},C^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
. However, this is insufficient to help the scientist before running the experiment. The scientist has
no
direct control over what the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
will be.

However, the distribution of the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
should depend on deterministic factors that the scientist can directly control, like the total number of droplets
{D}
, the expected number of cells per droplet
\lambda
, and the relative abundances
f^{(s)}
of the various strains
s
in the sampling population. The scientist would like to plan the experiment (
before
running it) in a way that allows them to (most likely) achieve certain thresholds of statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
for certain interactions of strains. Therefore the best way to help the scientist is to predict which values of statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
they will most likely be able to achieve as a function of the deterministic factors that the scientist can directly control. From that perspective it is inescapable that

(1)

the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
must be treated as random,

(2)

the most basic prerequisite for constraining the distribution of the statistical power
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})})
is to characterize the distributions of the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})},\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
, and

(3)

(if possible) the distributions of the treatment group and control group sizes
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
,
\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
should be described as functions of the deterministic factors that the scientist can directly control.

There is no need to incubate the droplets in order to estimate the probabilities (
8.5
). Indeed, it should be possible to estimate the probabilities using a much smaller number of total droplets
{D}
than what might be required in the final experiment to achieve certain (expected) statistical power thresholds. Therefore I propose that the scientist, to be able to design the experiment in a way that allows them achieve certain (expected) statistical power thresholds, first run a “
t=0
” version of the experiment that skips incubating the droplets and uses a smaller number of total droplets
{D}
. This both (i) maximizes the usefulness of the data for estimating the distributions of the treatment group and control group sizes (because it should mitigate artifacts resulting from microbial growth and competition, e.g. censoring) and (ii) decreases the cost (in money and time) of this “pre-experiment” to the scientist.

8.3
Non-Parametric Statistical Model

The non-parametric statistical model
\mathcal{M}
containing the distribution of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
for any of the
D
independent and identically distributed droplets is

\mathcal{M}=\left\{\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{P}\text{ is supported on }\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}^{S}\right\}\,.

(8.7)

8.4
Target Parameter Definitions

The below sections define two different classes of target parameters. Cf. section
2.1
for more details.

8.4.1
“Gluttonous” Target Parameters

The treatment group for inferring the effect of type
s_{1}
on the growth of type
s_{2}
corresponds to droplets where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
co-occur.
The set of all droplets satisfying this condition is

\begin{split}\mathcal{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{g}:=&\left\{d\in[{D}]:\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right\}\,.\end{split}

(8.8)

This is called the “
gluttonous
”
treatment
group because
all
droplets where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
co-occur are included.
Cf. figure
5.6
. Because of the i.i.d. assumption, this leads to the statistical queries
31
31

31

Cf. section
2.A
, in particular section
2.A.1
on the notation for functions used herein.
for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in[S]
,
s_{1}\not=s_{2}
:

\begin{split}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g}:&\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]\,,\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g}:&\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\,.\end{split}

(8.9)

The control group for inferring the effect of type
s_{1}
on the growth of type
s_{2}
corresponds to droplets where
s_{2}
occurs but
s_{1}
does not.
The set of all droplets satisfying this condition is

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{g}:=&\left\{d\in[{D}]:\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(0))\right\}\,.\end{split}

(8.10)

This is called the “
gluttonous
”
control
group because
all
droplets where
s_{2}
occurs but
s_{1}
does not are included. Cf. figure
5.6
. Because of the i.i.d. assumption, this leads to the statistical queries for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in[S]
,
s_{1}\not=s_{2}
:

\begin{split}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g}:&\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]\,,\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g}:&\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right)\,.\end{split}

(8.11)

These definitions place no constraints on the presence or absence of types
s\in[S]
besides
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
. This can be favorable for example when studying very rare types, for which there may be no or very few droplets containing
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
only, but some containing
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
along with other types. These definitions give us the best possible chance of avoiding “data starvation”.

8.4.2
“Picky” Target Parameters

The above “gluttonous” definitions correspond to more possible combinations of cell types than we probably would have included when studying the effect of type
s_{1}
on the growth of type
s_{2}
by manually plating combinations of cell types. This motivates the following definitions.

The set of droplets where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
co-occur in a way corresponding to the combination of cell types we would use if we were manually plating combinations of cell types is

\begin{split}\mathcal{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{p}:=&\left\{d\in[{D}]:\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right\}\,.\end{split}

(8.12)

This is called the “
picky
”
treatment
group because droplets where
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
co-occur are included
only when all other types are absent
. Cf. figure
5.6
. Because of the i.i.d. assumption, this leads to the statistical queries
32
32

32

Cf. section
2.A
, in particular section
2.A.1
on the notation for functions used herein.
for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in[S]
,
s_{1}\not=s_{2}
:

\begin{split}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p}:&\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]\,,\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p}:&\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\,.\end{split}

(8.13)

The set of droplets where
only

s_{2}
occurs,
and all other types are absent,
corresponding to the combination of cell types we would use if we were manually plating combinations of cell types, is

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{p}:=&\left\{d\in[{D}]:\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right\}\,.\end{split}

(8.14)

This
is called the “
picky
”
control
group
because droplets where
s_{2}
occurs but
s_{1}
does not occur are included
only when all other types are absent
. It is not enough for
s_{1}
alone to be absent.
Cf. figure
5.6
. Because of the i.i.d. assumption, this leads to the statistical queries for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in[S]
,
s_{1}\not=s_{2}
:

\begin{split}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p}:&\mathcal{M}\to[0,1]\,,\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p}:&\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right)\,.\end{split}

(8.15)

Note that unlike any of the other group definitions, this one is shared between all questions investigating effects on the growth of strain
s_{2}
.

Unlike the gluttonous groups, the picky groups
do
place constraints on the presence or absence of types
s\in[S]
besides
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
. For both the treatments and the controls, for “picky” groups all types besides
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
must have zero counts. Every picky group is a subset of its gluttonous counterpart.

The picky definitions can be favorable when we have plenty of droplets from which to make estimates. They reduce the possibility of confounding effects on the growth of type
s_{2}
that could be caused by types that are not type
s_{1}
. On the other hand, in instances where there are only very few or no droplets without “extra” types, for example for very rare types for which there are few droplets containing the type at all, the picky definitions could lead to “data starvation”.

8.5
Why Empirical Estimates are not Useful

The empirical estimates for these probabilities are inefficient for several reasons. What connects these reasons is the fact that the empirical estimates do not provide any way to connect these estimands with each other. Thus information about one estimand, which should ideally be applicable for learning about related estimands, is wasted. More specifically, note that we have a combinatorial explosion in the number of estimands as a function of the number of strains
S
. This is at least quadratic if we only consider the subsets of
S
corresponding to the groups defined above, and exponential if we want to consider the probabilities associated with arbitrary subsets of
[S]
(e.g. for the purpose of investigating higher-order interactions). Cf. section
7.3
, which discusses a related issue. This is in contrast to the number of parameters in the working models which (if including the relative abundances
f^{(s)}
) only grow linearly with the number of strains
S
. Thus using the empirical estimates leads to not only exponential increases in “description length” or “information” required to characterize the initial distribution of droplets, but also to exponential increases in computational runtime or algorithmic complexity for computing estimates of all estimands. The exponential number of parameters has practical implications. Cf. the problem motivating
[
TRKS+17
]
.

Related to this, especially for the treatment groups as well as types
s
with small relative abundances
f^{(s)}
, even when
{D}
is large, the expected counts can still be very small. In practice this leads to positivity violations, where the empirical estimates force us to state that certain unobserved events happen with zero probability when in principle information from related events should allow us make some small but non-zero estimate for their probabilities. This, combined with the combinatorial explosion, means that we would have to use very large values of
{D}
to make useful (i.e. non-zero and low-variance) estimates for all probabilities. Again, cf. the related discussion in section
7.3
. This is very inefficient because we would prefer to use a smaller or medium sized
{D}
for the initial trial run (the “
t=0
pre-experiment”, section
8
). Fitting a model for predicting data throughput is simply a prerequisite for estimating the power we will have to answer certain questions as a function of
{D}
, rather than the main question itself. Cf. again section
8.2
.

Similarly, the empirical estimates do not allow us to describe the probabilities in terms of parameters such as the relative abundances
f^{(s)}
or the mean number of cells per droplet. That means that, e.g. if we slightly modify our experiment by swapping out two strains with five different strains (which will obviously have different relative abundances), then when using empirical estimates we will have to re-estimate everything from scratch. In contrast, to the extent that parameters like the density concentration
\zeta_{D}
or compositional concentration
\zeta_{C}
are believed to correspond to “intrinsic features” of the experimental apparatus itself, we could reasonably believe that we could re-use estimates for them even if we change the number or relative abundances of strains or the mean number of cells per droplet.

8.6
Default Working Model: hPoMu

The “hierarchical Poisson Multinomial” (hPoMu) distributions
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\subset\mathcal{M}
are hierarchical distributions. The total number of cells in the droplet has a Poisson distribution, while (
conditional upon the total number of cells
) the numbers of cells belonging to the multiple types have a joint Multinomial distribution. Cf. figure
3.2
. Both choices of distribution correspond to assuming uniform and perfectly homogeneous sampling for each droplet. Cf. section
3.2
for two detailed arguments. The likelihood for the hPoMu distributions equals

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda,\vec{f})}\left(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{n}}\right)=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda,\vec{f})}\left(N(0)=n\right)\!\cdot\!\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda,\vec{f})}\left(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{n}}\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N(0)=n\right)\\
=&\hphantom{\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda,\vec{f})}\left(\right)}\frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{n}}{n!}\cdot\binom{n}{n^{(1)}\!\cdots\!n^{(S)}}\prod_{s=1}^{S}(f^{(s)})^{n^{(s)}}\,.\end{split}

(8.16)

An alternative form of the likelihood of hPoMu reveals that its marginal distributions are mutually independent Poisson distributions:

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda,\vec{f})}\left(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{n}}\right)=\prod_{s=1}^{S}\frac{e^{-f^{(s)}\lambda}(f^{(s)}\lambda)^{n^{(s)}}}{(n^{(s)})!}\,.\end{split}

(8.17)

Heuristically speaking, equation (
8.17
) says that during the formation of each droplet the expected number of cells, or “sampling rate”,
\lambda
is evenly spread out among the multiple types according to their frequencies in the population.

8.6.1
Projected Target Parameters Under hPoMu Working Model

Let
\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}:\mathcal{M}_{hPoMu}\to\mathcal{M}
,
\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}:\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathcal{P}
denote the inclusion map
33
33

33

Cf. section
2.A
, in particular the last part of section
2.A.2
.

{\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}}\mathcal{M}}
. Suppose
\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
is a projection
34
34

34

Cf. section
2.A
, in particular section
2.A.1
on the notation for functions used herein.
:

\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}:\mathcal{P}\mapsto\argmin_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}}\int_{\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}^{S}}L(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(\vec{n}))d\mathcal{P}(\vec{n})\,,

(8.18)

for a given loss function
L
, e.g.
L:p\mapsto-\log(p)
, onto the working parametric submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
from the nonparametric model
\mathcal{M}
. In particular one should have the relationships that
{\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}:\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}}
and that
{\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}}
is idempotent.

Given the projection of a distribution
\mathcal{P}
from the non-parametric model
\mathcal{M}
onto the working parametric submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
, we can now evaluate any of the non-parametrically defined parameters (
8.9
), (
8.11
), (
8.13
), (
8.15
) from section
8.4
at the projection, defining a new set of target parameters. This new set of target parameters corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
is defined below.

For a given
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}
, let
\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\mathcal{P}))=:\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
. The projected target parameters for the gluttonous treatment groups
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
are defined as (cf. section
2.4
)

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&(1-e^{-f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}})\cdot(1-e^{-f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}})\,,\end{array}

(8.19)

the projected target parameters for the gluttonous control groups
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
are defined as (cf. section
2.4
)

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right)\\
&&=&e^{-f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\cdot(1-e^{-f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}})\,,\end{array}

(8.20)

the projected target parameters for the picky treatment groups
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
are defined as (cf. section
2.3
)

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&(1-e^{-f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}})(1-e^{-f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}})\cdot e^{-(1-(f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}))\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\,,\end{array}

(8.21)

and the projected target parameters for the picky control groups
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}
are defined as (cf. section
2.3
)

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{hPoMu}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&(1-e^{-f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}})\cdot e^{-(1-f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\,.\end{array}

(8.22)

To estimate these, we just need to estimate
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}}
and then use the corresponding plugin estimators. Estimating
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}}
also gives us plugin estimators for the probabilities associated with
all

2^{S}
subsets of
[S]
. Thus “information is shared” between estimates for the probabilities of related events. Moreover, as long as all estimates of the
f^{(s)}_{\mathcal{P}}
are nonzero, all estimated probabilities will also all be nonzero. Therefore these estimates should both be “smoother” and more efficient than the empirical estimates, especially when
S
is large (corresponding to some of the
f^{(s)}_{\mathcal{P}}
being small). The large
S
regime would substantially advance the state of the art in microbial ecology, and thus is of particular interest.

8.6.2
Assumptions Justifying Default Working Model

Both of the two derivations justifying hPoMu as the default working model, cf. section
3.2
, make the following implicit assumptions (among others):

•

For
every
type
s
, the number of cells in the population which belong to type
s
is very large.

•

Each cell (regardless of its type) has the same small probability of ending up in the droplet as any other cell.

•

Whether a given cell (regardless of its type) ends up in the droplet is completely independent of what happens to any other cell.

The first can be thought of as stating that the population size for each type is effectively infinite. The second and third can together be thought of as stating that the sampling pool is perfectly homogeneous.

8.7
Failures of Default Working Model Assumptions

While hPoMu is a sensible working model to start with, its implicit assumptions failing to be realistic could undermine its usefulness in practice.

8.7.1
Populations are Finite

We assumed that
for each individual type
s
the number of cells is “effectively infinite”. For “rare” types it is a priori unclear whether this assumption is reasonable. However in practice, even for types with relative abundance as low as
0.01\%
, taking the finiteness of the populations into account does not lead to substantially different predictions than the default hPoMu working model. (Cf. sections
4.1
and
4.2.1
where this claim is substantiated.)

8.7.2
Density Heterogeneity

Different sections of the sampling pool could have average numbers of cells per unit volume that differ from the average number of cells per unit volume for the entire sampling pool (i.e. the total number of cells divided by the volume of the sampling pool). Herein I call this phenomenon “density heterogeneity”. Higher density heterogeneity implies more variance in the numbers of cells per unit volume throughout the sampling pool. Cf. figure
3.4
.

The default Poisson working model for the total number of cells per droplet can not account for additional variance that density heterogeneity might cause. Its variance is the smallest possible value that could hypothetically exist in practice, which seems unrealistic.

Because this describes extra variance in the total number of cells, regardless of their types, density heterogeneity is relevant even when there is only one type. Thus density heterogeneity is relevant even for the single-cell
35
35

35

The goal for these experiments is to have one cell per droplet, whence the name “single-cell”. However, because the droplet formation process is random, in practice some droplets in “single-cell” experiments may still end up with more than one cell.
, single type experiments which have already been intensively developed.

8.7.3
Compositional Heterogeneity

The relative abundance of any given cell type
s
across the entire sampling pool is a fixed value
f^{(s)}
. Different sections of the sampling pool could have relative abundances of the cell types that differ from those for the entire sampling pool. Herein I call this phenomenon “compositional heterogeneity”. Higher compositional heterogeneity implies more variance in the relative abundances for the cell types throughout the sampling pool. Cf. figure
3.5
.

The default Multinomial working model for the relative abundances of the cell types per droplet (
conditional upon the total number of cells
) does not account for additional variance that would be introduced by compositional heterogeneity. Its variance is the smallest possible value that could hypothetically exist in practice, which is seems unrealistic.

Compositional heterogeneity is relevant only for multi-cell, multi-type experiments. Such experiments are newer and still less common than single-cell, single type experiments. It seems that compositional heterogeneity may not have been identified before as a potential issue affecting the data throughput of droplet microfluidics experiments.

8.8
Working Model to Address Assumption Failures: ghNBDM

Any of the three assumptions from section
8.6.2
can be unrealistic in practice. As mentioned before, even for extremely rare strains, taking the finiteness of the populations into account (section
8.7.1
) does not lead to predictions substantially different than those made by the default hPoMu model. On the other hand, models for which the perfect homogeneity assumption is false
can
lead to substantially different predictions. This section introduces a generalization of the hPoMu working model that makes fewer homogeneity assumptions than hPoMu does. In particular, both nonzero density heterogeneity (section
8.7.2
) and nonzero compositional heterogeneity (section
8.7.3
) are allowed.

The “generalized hierarchical Negative Binomial Dirichlet-Multinomial” (ghNBDM) working model
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\subset\mathcal{M}
is a family of hierarchical distributions. The number of cells in the droplet has a Negative Binomial distribution with “density concentration” parameter
\zeta_{D}S
, while (
conditional upon the total number of cells
) the numbers of cells belonging to the multiple types have a joint Dirichlet-Multinomial distribution with “compositional concentration” parameter
\zeta_{C}S
. The likelihood for this parametric submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}
is

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda,\vec{f},\zeta_{D},\zeta_{C})}\left(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{n}}\right)\\
=&\quad\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda,\vec{f},\zeta_{D},\zeta_{C})}\left(N(0)=n\right)\hphantom{\frac{\lambda^{n}}{n!}}\!\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda,\vec{f},\zeta_{D},\zeta_{C})}\left(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{n}}\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N(0)=n\right)\\
=&\quad\frac{\Gamma(\zeta_{D}S+n)}{\Gamma(\zeta_{D}S)}\!\cdot\!\frac{(\zeta_{D}S)^{\zeta_{D}S}}{(\zeta_{D}S+\lambda)^{\zeta_{D}S+n}}\!\cdot\!\frac{\lambda^{n}}{n!}\!\cdot\!\frac{\Gamma(\zeta_{C}S)}{\Gamma(\zeta_{C}S+n)}\!\cdot\!\left[\prod_{s=1}^{S}\frac{\Gamma(\zeta_{C}Sf^{(s)}+n^{(s)})}{\Gamma\left(\zeta_{C}Sf^{(s)}\right)}\right]\!\cdot\!\binom{n}{n^{(1)}\!\cdots\!n^{(S)}}\,.\end{split}

(8.23)

In general the marginal distributions of members of the ghNBDM family
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\subset\mathcal{M}
have non-zero cross-covariance. Thus the entries
N^{(s)}(0)
of the random vector
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
will in general
not
be mutually independent. This is unlike the default working model (hPoMu)
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\subset\mathcal{M}
, for which the
N^{(s)}(0)
are mutually independent, cf. again section
8.6
, particularly equation (
8.17
).

Notice how this working model has two additional parameters compared to the hPoMu working model, namely the density concentration
\zeta_{D}
parameter and the compositional concentration
\zeta_{C}
parameter. Higher values of the density concentration
\zeta_{D}
parameter correspond to lower density heterogeneity (section
8.7.2
). As
\zeta_{D}\to\infty
the total number of cells in the droplet approaches a Poisson distribution. Cf. section
7.2.2
. Similarly, higher values of the compositional concentration
\zeta_{C}
parameter correspond to lower compositional heterogeneity (section
8.7.3
). As
\zeta_{C}\to\infty
the joint distribution of the numbers of cells belonging to the multiple types approaches a Multinomial distribution. Cf. section
6.2.2
. The hPoMu model is approached in the limit as both
\zeta_{D}\to\infty
and
\zeta_{C}\to\infty
jointly. Cf. figure
3.6
and section
8.1
.
For details about the calculations demonstrating how the somewhat complicated expressions involving the
\Gamma
function do indeed generalize the likelihoods of the Poisson and Multinomial distributions, cf. sections
7.2
,
6.2
, and
3.A
. At a high level, the idea is to use Stirling’s approximation, in particular the formulations given by
[
Rob55
]
and
[
Gor94
]
.

Projected Target Parameters Under ghNBDM Working Model

This is similar to what was done in section
8.6.1
. See section
2.B.1
for details.

8.9
Intermediate Choice: hNBDM Working Model

Given estimates of
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}
, and
{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}
, plugin estimators for the above target parameters (
2.B.3
), (
2.B.4
), (
2.B.5
), (
2.B.6
) can be computed to arbitrary accuracy, e.g. by truncating terms of the series expansions. Nevertheless, it is at best tedious to derive explicit bounds on the accuracy as a function of the number of terms in the truncated series expansions. An alternative is to use the “hierarchical Negative Binomial Dirichlet-Multinomial” (hNBDM) working model
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\subset\mathcal{M}
.

The hNBDM working model is intermediate in complexity and flexibility between the ghNBDM and hPoMu working models, indeed
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hPoMu}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}
. Distributions in the hNBDM working model are distributions in the ghNBDM working model such that the density concentration
\zeta_{D}
and compositional concentration
\zeta_{C}
parameters are equal,
\zeta_{D}=\zeta_{C}=:\zeta
. Hence the hNBDM working model has one more parameter than the hPoMu working model, but also one less parameter than the ghNBDM working model.

The real “magic” of the hNBDM working model happens in its likelihood: when
\zeta_{D}=\zeta_{C}
, the resulting cancellations and simplifications in the ghNBDM likelihood cause it to factorize over its marginal distributions:

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}(\lambda,\vec{f},\zeta)}\left(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{n}}\right)\\
=&\quad\prod_{s=1}^{S}\frac{\Gamma(\zeta Sf^{(s)}+n^{(s)})}{\Gamma(\zeta Sf^{(s)})}\frac{(\zeta Sf^{(s)})^{\zeta Sf^{(s)}}}{(\zeta Sf^{(s)}+f^{(s)}\lambda)^{\zeta Sf^{(s)}+n^{(s)}}}\frac{(f^{(s)}\lambda)^{n^{(s)}}}{(n^{(s)})!}\,.\end{split}

(8.24)

In other words, the marginal distributions are mutually independent Negative Binomial distributions. The “sampling rate”
\lambda
is proportionately “spread out” across the marginals according to the frequencies
f^{(s)}
, exactly analogous to what occurs for the hPoMu working model. Indeed, the hPoMu distributions can be considered the “boundary” of the hNBDM model reached by the limit
\zeta\to\infty
.

Projected Target Parameters Under hNBDM Working Model

This is similar to what was done in section
8.6.1
. See section
2.B.2
for details.

9
Conclusion

Findings and Contributions

The data from the noisy dynamical systems corresponding to each droplet can be understood as censored observations of a multivariate Markov process.
The statistical understanding of a droplet’s initial state is crucial to overcoming the main limitation of these experiments, the uncontrolled assignment of microbes to droplets.

Practical Implications

Giving explicit descriptions of the intended underlying statistical model has several benefits. By formalizing intuitive insights, like the division of the experiment into three phases, it allows us use those insights as the basis for data analysis. It helps to clarify the similarities and differences between this problem and other problem, as well the similarities and differences between methods used for this problem and methods used for other problems. It makes explicit which assumptions we are making when analyzing the data, so that we might later question those assumptions if necessary. It guides us to understanding clearly what the most challenging aspects of the data analysis are. In short, it gives us a starting point that anchors everything else we might do.

Next Steps and Open Questions

There are several different choices we could have made when positing the explicit statistical models given in this chapter. It remains to be fully investigated what the consequences of such different choices would be. Reasonable possible choices, and their consequences, are explained in the next part. Later, methods for inferring interactions that would apply to observations produced by these models are considered.

Chapter 2. Appendices

\namedsubappendices

Appendix 2.A
Conventions and Notation for Functions

Section
2.A.1
discusses the notation used for functions. Section
2.A.2
discusses the convention used for function composition.

2.A.1
Function Notation

Herein I use a definition of function that requires not only (i) a rule of assignment, but also (ii) a specified domain (source) and codomain (target). A notion of function requiring only (i) corresponds more to “classical” mathematics, and in some sources is called an “intensional” definition of function
[
Sel13
, section 1.1]
. In contrast, a notion of function requiring (i) and (ii) corresponds more to “modern” mathematics, and in some sources is called an “extensional” definition of function
[
Sel13
, section 1.1]
. Herein I only use the ‘‘extensional’’ notion
36
36

36

In programming language terminology, the “intensional” notion is “untyped”, whereas the “extensional” notion is “typed”. Thus one motivation for using the latter notion more often in “modern” mathematics is that the resulting statements are more “type safe”.

.

Given a function
f
whose domain is a set
X
and whose codomain is a set
Y
, thus sending every element
x\in X
to a unique element
f(x)\in Y
, I write

\begin{split}f:&X\to Y\,,\\
f:&x\mapsto f(x)\,.\end{split}

(2.A.1)

The first line,
f:X\to Y
, indicates the domain and codomain of the function. The second line (using “anonymous function” notation from lambda calculus),
f:x\mapsto f(x)
, indicates the rule of assignment of the function.

In particular, I use the convention that for, two functions
f_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{1}
and
f_{2}:X_{2}\to Y_{2}
to be equal, not only must they have the same rule of assignment, but they also must have the same domain and codomain. For example, the function
f_{1}:x\mapsto x
and
f_{2}:x\mapsto x
are considered different when
f_{1}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f_{2}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, even though they both have the same rule of assignment and same domain, because their codomains are different. With this convention, a rule of assignment is insufficient to specify a function.

2.A.2
Conventions for Function Composition

There are at least two different possible conventions for when, given two functions

f_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{1}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad f_{2}:X_{2}\to Y_{2}\,,

we say that the composition of the two functions,
{f_{2}\circ f_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{2}}
, is defined:

(i)

whenever
Y_{1}\subseteq X_{2}
,

(ii)

whenever
Y_{1}=X_{2}
.

Using either convention, the rule of assignment of
f_{2}\circ f_{1}
is
x_{1}\mapsto f_{2}(f_{1}(x_{1}))
. However, with the first convention, there are potentially infinitely many functions
f_{1}
such that
f_{2}\circ f_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{2}
is defined and has the same rule of assignment, each corresponding to a distinct
Y_{1}\subseteq X_{2}
. In particular, the first convention corresponds to defining a function only in terms of a rule of assignment, and not requiring that a specific domain and codomain be specified.

Hence the first convention for composition of functions is ill-suited for the definition of function explained in section
2.A.1
above. It is incompatible with the demand that not only the rule of assignment of a function must be specified but also its domain and codomain.

The second convention for composition of functions is compatible with the definition of function explained in section
2.A.1
above. The second convention requires a specific codomain for
f_{1}
(namely exactly the domain of
f_{2}
) is specified in order for the composition
f_{2}\circ f_{1}
to even be defined. This convention is less ambiguous and is the convention I will use.

The choice between the two conventions can have practical consequences. For example, when using the first convention, the composition of two surjective (a.k.a. onto) functions need not again be surjective, and the composition of two bijections need not again be a bijection. This can be subtle and confusing and lead to hard to detect mistakes. On the other hand, when using the second convention, the composition of two surjective functions is always surjective, and the composition of two bijections is always a bijection.

For example, say that
f_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{1}
and
f_{2}:X_{2}\to Y_{2}
are both surjective, with
Y_{1}\subsetneq X_{2}
. If the restriction of
f_{2}
to
Y_{1}
is not surjective (onto)
Y_{2}
, then, when using the first convention, the composition
f_{2}\circ f_{1}
will not be surjective, even though
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both surjective. However, when using the second convention, the composition
f_{2}\circ f_{1}
is not even defined. Instead, we first need to define the inclusion function
\iota:Y_{1}\xhookrightarrow{}X_{2}
,
\iota:y_{1}\mapsto y_{1}
, which is clearly injective but not surjective because
Y_{1}\subsetneq X_{2}
by assumption. Then the function
X_{1}\to Y_{2}
we are interested in is
f_{2}\circ\iota\circ f_{1}
. It becomes clear then why the composite function is not surjective. Although
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both surjective,
\iota
is not.

Appendix 2.B
Additional Projected Target Parameters

Section
2.B.1
discusses the analogue of section
8.6.1
but for the ghNBDM working model. Section
2.B.2
does likewise but for the hNBDM working model.

2.B.1
Projected Target Parameters Under ghNBDM Working Model

Let
\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:\mathcal{M}_{ghNBDM}\to\mathcal{M}
,
\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:\mathcal{P}\mapsto\mathcal{P}
denote the inclusion map
37
37

37

Cf. section
2.A
, in particular the last part of section
2.A.2
.

{\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}}\mathcal{M}}\,.

Suppose
\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}
is a projection
38
38

38

Cf. section
2.A
, in particular section
2.A.1
on the notation for functions used herein.
:

\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:\mathcal{P}\mapsto\argmin_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}}\int_{\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}^{S}}L(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(\vec{n}))d\mathcal{P}(\vec{n})\,,

(2.B.1)

for a given loss function
L
, e.g.
L:p\mapsto-\log(p)
, onto the working parametric submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}
from the nonparametric model
\mathcal{M}
. One should have the relationships:

•

{\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}}
, and

•

{\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}}

is idempotent.

Given the projection of a distribution
\mathcal{P}
from the non-parametric model
\mathcal{M}
onto the working parametric submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}
, we can now evaluate any of the non-parametrically defined parameters (
8.9
), (
8.11
), (
8.13
), (
8.15
) from section
8.4
at the projection, defining a new set of target parameters. This new set of target parameters corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}
is defined below.

I use the notation
(x)^{\overline{n}}
ostensibly popularized by Knuth for the
rising factorial
,

{(x)^{\overline{n}}:=\prod_{\nu=0}^{n-1}(x+i)=\frac{\Gamma(x+n)}{\Gamma(x)}}\,,

(2.B.2)

assuming
x>0
, real, and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
. Cf. footnote
28
of section
2.2
.

For a given
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}
, let
\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))=:\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}})\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}
. The projected target parameters for the gluttonous treatment groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\!\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S}}{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S\!+\!\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S\!+\!n}}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}{({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{n}}{n!}\left[({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}\!-\!({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}))^{\overline{n}}\right.\\
&&&\displaystyle\left.\hphantom{\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{(\zeta_{D}S)^{\zeta_{D}S}}{(\zeta_{D}S\!+\!\lambda)^{\zeta_{D}S\!+\!n}}\frac{(\zeta_{D}S)^{\overline{n}}}{(\zeta_{C}S)^{\overline{n}}}\frac{\lambda^{n}}{n!}}\!-\!({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}))^{\overline{n}}\!+\!({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}))^{\overline{n}}\right]\,,\end{array}

(2.B.3)

the projected target parameters for the gluttonous control groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\!\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S}}{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S\!+\!\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S\!+\!n}}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}{({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{n}}{n!}\,\,\cdot\\
&&&\displaystyle\hphantom{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}}\left[({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}))^{\overline{n}}\!-\!({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}))^{\overline{n}}\right]\,,\end{array}

(2.B.4)

the projected target parameters for the picky treatment groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S}}{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S\!+\!\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S+n}}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}{({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{n}}{n!}\left[({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S(f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\!+\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}))^{\overline{n}}\right.\\
&&&\displaystyle\hphantom{\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S}}{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S\!+\!\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S+n}}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}{({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{n}}{n!}}\left.-({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}})^{\overline{n}}-({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})^{\overline{n}}\right]\,,\end{array}

(2.B.5)

and the projected target parameters for the picky control groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{ghNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{ghNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}},{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S}}{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S+\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})^{{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S+n}}\frac{({\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}{({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}S)^{\overline{n}}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{n}}{n!}({\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})^{\overline{n}}\,.\end{array}

(2.B.6)

To estimate these, we just need to estimate
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}
, and
{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}
and then use the corresponding plugin estimators. Estimating
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
{\zeta_{D}}_{,\mathcal{P}}
, and
{\zeta_{C}}_{,\mathcal{P}}
also gives us plugin estimators for the probabilities associated with
all

2^{S}
subsets of
[S]
. So again, like for the hPoMu working model, “information is shared” between estimates for the probabilities of related events. Again, like for the hPoMu working model, as long as all estimates of the
f^{(s)}_{\mathcal{P}}
are nonzero, all estimated probabilities will also all be nonzero. Thus these estimates should again be useful particularly for the important large
S
regime.

Projected Target Parameters Under hNBDM Working Model

Let
\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}:\mathcal{M}_{hNBDM}\to\mathcal{M}

For a given
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{M}
, let
\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))=:\operatorname{hNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta}_{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}
. The projected target parameters for the gluttonous treatment groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},g,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\left(1-\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)\cdot\left(1-\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)\,,\end{array}

(2.B.8)

the projected target parameters for the gluttonous control groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},g,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\}\,,\,s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}}\cdot\left(1-\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)\,,\end{array}

(2.B.9)

the projected target parameters for the picky treatment groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{T},p,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\left(1-\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)\cdot\left(1-\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)\cdot\\
&&&\displaystyle\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})+(1\!-\!f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}S(1-f^{(s_{1})}_{\mathcal{P}}-f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})}\,,\end{array}

(2.B.10)

and the projected target parameters for the picky control groups

\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:=\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p}\circ\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}\circ\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}

are defined as

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{M}&\to&[0,1]\\
&&&\\
\Psi^{(s_{1},s_{2})}_{\mathcal{C},p,\operatorname{hNBDM}}:&\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&\mathbb{P}_{\iota_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\Pi_{\operatorname{hNBDM}}(\mathcal{P}))}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right)\\
&&=&\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{hNBDM}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}},{\zeta}_{\mathcal{P}})}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right)\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\left(1-\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}+f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}Sf^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)\cdot\\
&&&\displaystyle\left(\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})}{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}S(1\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})+(1\!-\!f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}\right)^{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}S(1-f^{(s_{2})}_{\mathcal{P}})}\,.\end{array}

(2.B.11)

Note that (cf. Lemma
3.3
from section
3.A
) as
\zeta\to\infty
:

\lim_{\zeta\to\infty}\left(\frac{\zeta Sf^{(s)}}{\zeta Sf^{(s)}+f^{(s)}\lambda}\right)^{\zeta Sf^{(s)}}=e^{-f^{(s)}\lambda}\,,

(2.B.12)

and therefore equations (
2.B.8
), (
2.B.9
), (
2.B.10
), (
2.B.11
)
do
approach equations (
8.19
), (
8.20
), (
8.21
), (
8.22
) respectively in the limit as
\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}\to\infty
, as we would have expected a priori. To estimate (
2.B.8
), (
2.B.9
), (
2.B.10
), (
2.B.11
), we just need to estimate
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}}
, and
{\zeta}_{\mathcal{P}}
and then use the corresponding plugin estimators. Again, estimating
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\vec{f}_{\mathcal{P}}
, and
{\zeta}_{\mathcal{P}}
also gives us plugin estimators for the probabilities associated with
all

2^{S}
subsets of
[S]
. Thus like for the hPoMu and ghNBDM working models, again “information is shared” between estimates for the probabilities of related events and all estimated probabilities will be nonzero as long as all estimates of the
f^{(s)}_{\mathcal{P}}
are nonzero. While the
\zeta_{D}=\zeta_{C}
constraint provides less flexibility than the full ghNBDM working model, it still has both more flexibility than the hPoMu working model and plugin estimators with the same low level of complexity as those of the hPoMu working model.

Appendix 2.C
Toy Model

Consider the following toy model. Droplets have one cell with
50\%
probability, two cells with two different strains with
50\%
probability. In both cases the distribution is assumed Multinomial with parameters corresponding to population relative abundances. Now consider the effect of strain
s_{1}
, with relative abundance
0.01\%
, on strain
s_{2}
, with relative abundance
20\%
. If the total number of droplets is
{D}=500,000
, then we expect
50,000
droplets containing strain
s_{2}
only, whereas we would expect only
(50\%\times 20\%\times 0.01\%)\times 500,000=5
droplets containing both strains. Cf. section
7.3
which expresses a similar idea.

Keep in mind that the reality is actually almost always
worse
than this toy model. For example, there will be a large and non-negligible number of empty droplets with zero cells. Moreover, when droplets are non-empty, all of the simplifying assumptions implicitly made above can and often will fail to be true. When any given strain is present, it’s possible for there to be more than one cell belonging to that strain in the droplet (the “multiple representatives problem”, cf. section
5.C
). When the droplet is non-empty, there can be more than two strains present in the droplet. Finally, even when conditioning only on droplets with either one or two strains present, there usually will not be a
1:1
ratio of droplets with one strain versus droplets with two strains.

Even the
1:1
ratio from the toy model isn’t ideal, because there are
O(S^{2})
possible two-strain treatment groups, and
O(S)
possible one-strain control groups, so actually in an ideal world we would like a
\approx 1:S
ratio of droplets with one strain versus droplets with two strains. So even for the toy model, the sizes of the treatment groups compared to the sizes of the control groups would become increasingly imbalanced as the number of strains becomes larger. This is problematic to the extent that we want
S
as large as possible.

Part II
Modelling Initial Formation of Droplets

Inferring ecological models of complex microbial communities entails understanding the interactions between microbes and how they affect each other’s growth. The initial counts of cells from each strain within a droplet are the baseline for comparing the growth of microbes under different conditions. Being better able to better characterize this baseline for growth means being better able to characterize effects on growth. Thus modelling and understanding these initial counts will help us to understand microbial interactions.

The broader field for part
II
is the study of statistical models for multivariate count data. One common approach for defining and categorizing these involves generalized linear models. Cf.
[
KTS+15
]
or
[
ZZZS17
]
. Hierarchical models, often based in some way on the Poisson distribution (which is important for univariate count data), are also commonly used. Cf.
[
IYAR17
]
for a review. Models for multivariate count data are important to many problems, such as topic modelling. See
[
Zho18
]
or
[
KTS+15
]
for examples.

Part
II
concerns more particularly statistical models for multivariate count data arising from two sources: high-throughput biological assays and ecological studies.

For multivariate count data arising from high-throughput biological assays, such models have been considered for (single-cell) droplet-based microfluidics experiments, and for RNA-seq experiments. Generally the Poisson distribution is used as the default statistical model for single-cell droplet-based microfluidics experiments
[
CNd+15
]
. (Although this is univariate count data, not multivariate. See any of
[
LE13
]
,
[
KvVDH10
]
,
[
MRH20
]
, or
[
GRHW12
]
for reviews of such experiments.) Examples of work considering statistical models for characterizing count data from RNA-seq experiments include
[
YHV13
]
and
[
ZZZS17
]
.

The literature for multivariate count data arising from ecological studies is likely more developed. Relevant studies are often described as investigating “joint species abundances” or “joint species distribution”. See
[
TRKS+17
]
,
[
OTN+17
]
, and
[
CMR21
]
. Work such as
[
BGF01
]
has considered the possible effects of preferential (dis)association of certain organisms (cf. section
6.1.2
). Such work is not limited only to macroecology. Studies such as
[
BML+15
]
,
[
YS14
]
,
[
HCSB20
]
, or
[
MWC19
]
investigate count data from microbial ecology.

Specific Problem

The next four chapters of this thesis discuss the “
t=0
experiment”. This means focusing on candidate statistical working models for the first phase of the overall experiment described in section
4
. Chapter
3
defines and discusses the candidate statistical working models. Chapter
4
qualitatively compares the candidate statistical working models. We want to determine whether any behave substantially differently from the default working model, which is the simplest. Motivated by the results of chapter
4
, chapter
5
investigates and compares the target estimands for each of the candidate statistical working models. We want to determine whether substantially different behavior between the candidate working models translates into substantially different values of the target estimands in practice. Chapter
6
constructs estimators and obtains inference for all of the candidate statistical working models. Even under model misspecification, these estimators return sensible results.

Chapter
3

Chapter
3
belongs to the general field of describing and categorizing statistical models for multivariate count data.
For any particular problem with multivariate count data, some thought is usually needed to find reasonable models.
Chapter
3
investigates which statistical (working) models are reasonable choices for describing the initial state of a droplet.
Chapter
3
shows that specific assumptions justify a default working model for this problem.
New working models are derived by relaxing each of these assumptions.

Chapter
4

The problem considered in chapter
4
falls under the general field of “model selection”.
Herein we only need to consider the subfield of so-called “goodness of fit” tests, in particular those using the classic likelihood ratio statistic.
Concretely, chapter
4
investigates whether the failure of any of the assumptions identified in chapter
3
leads to substantially new behavior, and
demonstrates that log likelihood ratio statistics can be used to address this question.
Failure of the sampling without replacement assumption turns out to have negligible effects in practice, but failures of the other assumptions could be important.

Chapter
5

Like chapter
4
, the problem of chapter
5
also belongs both to the general field of model selection and to the subfield of goodness of fit tests in particular.
However, the focus herein is on goodness of fit tests for contingency tables using the
\chi^{2}
-divergence test statistic. (Cf. section
2.2
for terminology.)
Chapter
5
investigates how failures of the assumptions identified in chapter
3
affect the targeted estimands from section
8.4
.
The results of this chapter confirm that more severe failures of these assumptions lead to more severe discrepancies with the predictions derived from the default working model.
The nature of the effect depends on the chosen grouping of droplets defining the targeted estimands.

Chapter
6

The problem of chapter
6
belongs to the general field of point estimation.
Chapter
6
is concerned in particular with the plug-in (also known as “method of moments”) and maximum likelihood approaches to point estimation.
The chapter investigates how to measure, from the data produced by unincubated droplets, failures of the assumptions identified in chapter
3
. This is framed as a point estimation problem.
The chapter presents both plugin and maximum likelihood estimators for solving this problem.
In doing so, failures of the violations are also shown to be understandable non-parametrically.

Significance

We have to investigate specific methods (cf. part
III
) to predict how much data is needed to infer microbial interactions. To configure the experiment in accordance with such predictions, we first need to predict how much usable data will be produced. (Cf. again the discussion from section
8.2
.) Part
II
helps with the latter kind of prediction. Running the experiment once without incubating the droplets allows us to predict how much usable data will be produced by understanding the initial distribution of cells within droplets. This includes assumptions for the distribution of the total numbers of cells within droplets, and for the distribution (given the total number of cells) of proportions of each strain.

Chapter 3
[

Candidate Statistical Working Models for Initial Formation of Droplets]Motivation for and Definitions of Candidate Statistical Working Models for the Initial Formation of Droplets

Herein I show that specific assumptions justify a default working model for the initial state of droplets. See section
3.3
. I derive new working models by relaxing each of these assumptions. See sections
5
,
6
,
7
, and
8
. I discuss concrete ways these assumptions could fail to be true in practice, so the relaxations are not purely hypothetical. See section
4
.

Section
1
provides context for what follows. Section
2
proposes a general framework for defining any statistical model of the initial formation of droplets. Section
3
proposes a “default” working model of the initial formation of droplets. Section
4
explains three ways the assumptions underlying this default working model could fail to be true in practice.

The rest of chapter explains how to model these assumption failures. Section
5
proposes a working model accounting for the finiteness of the microbial population. Section
6
proposes a working model accounting for heterogeneity in the relative abundances of microbial strains throughout the microbial population. Section
7
proposes a working model accounting for heterogeneity in the average number of cells throughout the microbial population. Section
8
describes a family of distributions subsuming both of the previous two working models as well as the default working model. Section
9
summarizes the chapter.

1
Background and Significance

Broader field

This chapter belongs to the general field of describing and categorizing statistical models for multivariate count data. The references
[
Zho18
]
and
[
KTS+15
]
discuss some examples of these in the field of topic modelling. The article
[
IYAR17
]
is a helpful review of some other relevant statistical models. Approaches related to generalized linear models are often used to define statistical models for multivariate count data, cf.
[
ZZZS17
]
and
[
KTS+15
]
for examples. See also the discussion earlier from the introduction to Part
II
.

In general, some thought is required to find models that are reasonable for a given, specific problem involving multivariate count data. The references
[
BGF01
]
,
[
BML+15
]
,
[
OTN+17
]
, and
[
CMR21
]
are examples of statistical models tailored to problems from ecological studies. Similarly, the references
[
YHV13
]
,
[
CNd+15
]
, and
[
ZZZS17
]
are examples of statistical models tailored to the analysis of data from high-throughput biological assays. (See any of
[
LE13
]
,
[
KvVDH10
]
,
[
MRH20
]
, or
[
GRHW12
]
for reviews of some examples of such experiments.)

Specific problem

Herein I investigate which statistical (working) models can be used to describe a droplet’s initial state. A statistical model of the initial formation of droplets helps us to characterize microbial interactions. One of the major challenges of the MOREI data is the randomness of the initial droplet formation process. Unlike manually plating everything, with MOREI the scientist has no
direct
control over which microbial interactions are observed. This means a loss of control over which questions that can be answered from the data. The premise of MOREI is that the vastly increased throughput makes the tradeoff of decreased control worthwhile. The more control we can retain for the scientist when using MOREI, compared to direct plating, the stronger the case for its superiority over existing methods, and the greater its ability to advance the state of the art. Cf. figure
3.1
.

Figure 3.1
:
Control-Throughput Tradeoff Curve. Having a predictive statistical model of initial droplet formation would allow us to increase control without sacrificing throughput, moving us to the higher tradeoff curve.

To restore the scientist’s control over which questions they can answer using MOREI data, we want to account for the effects which the randomness of the initial droplet formation process has on the data. To account for these effects, we need to make accurate predictions about what these effects will be. Cf. again section
8.2
. To make such predictions, we need to understand the randomness of the initial droplet formation process. To understand its randomness, we need to have adequate statistical models for describing the initial droplet formation process. Similar previous work involving the selection of statistical models for count data has helped to improve the design of RNA-seq experiments, see e.g.
[
YHV13
]
or
[
ZZZS17
]
.
I hope that this work is an initial step towards analogous progress in the design of MOREI and related future experiments.

The ultimate goal of making accurate predictions about the droplet formation process may sound overly ambitious. Yet the amount of throughput that we can potentially achieve with a MOREI experiment is enormous.
