1
Background and Significance

Broader field

The broader field chapter
7
belongs to is the inference of ecological models for microbial interactions. The goal is to use data produced by the MOREI experiment to infer these. See
[
FR12
]
for a review of how networks are often used as such ecological models, and
[
XAF+17
]
(or section
2
) for how signed networks in particular can be used to describe microbial interactions. Cf. the discussion earlier from the introduction to Part
III
.

The subfield that chapter
7
considers is the use of relative fitness measurements to quantify microbial interactions. (The ecological model is then a signed network whose weights are the corresponding relative fitness values. Cf. section
3
or the discussion of “competition assays”
[
WL15
]
from the introduction of Part
I
.) There are many distinct estimands used as quantitative measures of “(relative) fitness” found throughout the literature. Cf. the definitions found in
[
WPW+15
]
,
[
WL15
]
,
[
BEG+18
]
, or
[
HRM+19
]
for several distinct examples. Definitions are often stated implicitly or imprecisely, contributing to the difficulty of comparing definitions across papers and of analyzing associated statistical problems.

Specific problem

Herein I investigate how a particular measure of relative fitness can be recast into the statistical framework of average treatment effects. The underlying idea behind this measure is easy to motivate heuristically and very simple (cf. section
7.C
). Similar features of other definitions of relative fitness perhaps encourage their primarily informal treatment in the literature. In any case, by making this definition precise (cf. section
3
), analysis of the corresponding statistical problems becomes much clearer. In particular, one can readily see that this definition admits an interpretation as an “average treatment effect” estimand. Cf.
[
vdLR11
, chapter 10]
for more about the general theory of average treatment effects, which is substantially well-developed.

At least two papers in the virology literature that study
4
4

4

As opposed to the “unpartitioned interactions” that we are interested in, cf. the introduction to Part
I
for terminology.

“bipartite interactions”,
[
MKBDB00
]
and
[
HdlTCD91
]
, appear to use the same (or a sufficiently analogous) definition of relative fitness as that used in this chapter (cf. section
7.B.5.1
for the latter.) This is such a straightforward definition that most likely other examples of its use exist in the literature, including those studying non-viral microbes and/or “unpartitioned interactions”. However, I have not yet been able to find additional examples, in part due to how definitions of “fitness” in the literature are usually discussed only imprecisely or even implicitly (as in e.g.
[
HdlTCD91
]
).

Particular approach

Being able to formulate this problem in terms of average treatment effects suggests it should be possible to apply targeted minimum loss based estimation (TMLE)
[
vdLR11
]
to this problem.
This chapter demonstrates how one can follow the first two stages of the targeted learning roadmap
[
vdLR11
, section 1.5]
with this choice of estimand: (1) defining the research question, and (2) estimation. Hence this chapter can be thought of as a “proof of concept” for applying TMLE, but future work will fill out all of the details of completing the targeted learning roadmap from start to finish.

Because PhenoPath
[
CY18
]
was designed for a problem involving fairly high-throughput data produced using microfluidics, and which is not strictly longitudinal, attempting to modify it to apply to the problem of inferring microbial interactions from MOREI data seems like a natural approach, and more sophisticated than using these simple relative fitness estimates. However, although PhenoPath was suitably modified to apply to this problem, the results of preliminary work suggest that PhenoPath does not work well for this problem.

One of the reasons the method from
[
CY18
]
failed to work well on (simulated) data for this problem in preliminary work is because it was designed for “gene
\times
environment” interactions, not “gene
\times
gene” interactions. (Cf. again item
(4)
from section
6.1
for terminology.) Its computational performance for this latter, more computationally demanding, problem proved inadequate. (If
n
is the number of genes, the latter problem corresponds to
n
instances of the former problem, cf. footnote
18
from section
6.1
.)

The method from
[
CY18
]
also seemed unable to handle the sparsity of the data well, much less exploit it. One reason seems to be that the model implemented by
[
CY18
]
inherently treats
0
values as “biological zeros”, when in fact for this problem they are almost all “sampling zeros” (cf. the terminology from section
7.A
and quoted from
[
PGB21
]
). More often than not, when a given strain is not found within a given droplet, the reason is because the strain was never in the droplet in the first place. Yet PhenoPath appears to always interpret this the same way as if the strain had been initially present in the droplet and later killed off by those strains that were found in the droplet.

2
Full Data Model

The full data model
\mathcal{M}^{F}
corresponds to the likelihoods from equations (
7.5
), (
7.8
), and (
7.12
). This can be interpreted as the marginalization of a continuous time Markov process describing the growth and death of cells over time. The full data model
\mathcal{M}^{F}
assumes for any given droplet not only that
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t)
is known, but also that
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
is known as well.

3
Full Data Average Treatment Effect (ATE) Definitions

For the full data model
\mathcal{M}^{F}
, denote any average treatment effect (ATE) full data estimand (i.e. assuming that
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
is known) corresponding to the effect of strain
5
5

5

Herein I use “strains” to refer equally to strains belonging to the same species(/genus/family/etc.) as well as to strains belonging to different species(/genera/families/etc.), because the distinction is irrelevant for setting up the abstract problem. It may matter for the implementation of a specific experiment.

s_{1}
on
s_{2}
at time
t
by
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
.
In these definitions of “average treatment effect”, the term corresponding to the treatment group can be interpreted heuristically as the “numerator” of a log-ratio, whereas the term corresponding to the control group can be interpreted heuristically as the “denominator” of the same log-ratio. Cf. section
7.C
. This heuristic interpretation influences the choice of notation used below. Also note that it is implicit that any choice of treatment group and control group do not overlap. Thus the averages corresponding to the two terms of the ATE definition are always taken over two disjoint sets of droplets.

In defining the average treatment effects, note that (as before) we have at least
2
choices (“picky” or “gluttonous”) of treatment groups and at least
2
choices (“picky” or “gluttonous”) of control groups. Cf. again section
8.4
, section
3
, or section
2.1
. ATE estimands using a “picky” treatment group will be denoted
\Psi^{F}_{p/\cdot}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
(“p” for “
p
icky”), while ATE estimands using a “gluttonous” treatment group will be denoted
\Psi^{F}_{g/\cdot}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
(“g” for “
g
luttonous”). Likewise, ATE estimands using a “picky” control group will be denoted
\Psi^{F}_{\cdot/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
(“p” for “
p
icky”), while ATE estimands using a “gluttonous” control group will be denoted
\Psi^{F}_{\cdot/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
(“g” for “
g
luttonous”). To summarize, within the above framework there are two ways to define the treatment group term of the ATE, and two ways to define the control group term of the ATE, leading to
2\times 2=4
total possible distinct definitions of ATE (average treatment effect) under consideration. Explicit definitions for all
4
of these possible choices of full data ATE estimand are given below.

(Full Data)
Picky treatment, picky control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{F}_{p/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{F}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi^{F}_{p/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{F}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\\
\end{array}

(3.1)

(Full Data)
Picky treatment, gluttonous control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{F}_{p/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{F}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi^{F}_{p/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{F}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\},s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right]\end{array}

(3.2)

(Full Data)
Gluttonous treatment, picky control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{F}_{g/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{F}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi^{F}_{g/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{F}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\end{array}

(3.3)

(Full Data)
Gluttonous treatment, gluttonous control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{F}_{g/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{F}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi^{F}_{g/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{F}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\{s_{2}\},s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\right]\end{array}

(3.4)

It turns out (cf. section
8
) that these estimands are identifiable from the observed data provided we make the following assumptions:

•

we always have that
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))
,

•

the marginal distributions of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
, i.e. the distributions of
N^{(1)}(0)
,
N^{(2)}(0)
, …,
N^{(S)}(0)
, are all mutually independent.

These conditions are sufficient, but they may not be necessary.

3.1
Stratified Full Data Average Treatment Effect

The motivation for using the following definition is explained in section
7
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{F}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{F}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)=1,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)\right]\right.\\
&&&\hphantom{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\right]}\left.-\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)=0,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)\right]\right]\\
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\mathbf{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{S-2}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)=1,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right]\right.\\
&&&\displaystyle\left.-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)=0,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right]\right]\!\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right)\,.\end{array}

(3.5)

Observe how this definition implicitly uses a definition of treatment and covariates (cf. section
7
) that ignores the multiple representatives problem (cf. section
5.C
). The analogous estimand that would correspond to not ignoring the multiple representatives problem appears to not (even in principle) be identifiable from the observed data. By ignoring the multiple representatives problem, we allow ourselves to use the binary strain presence/absence vector
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
(cf. section
2.1.1
), instead of the full strain count vector
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
, to define the treatment and covariates. Considering that already fairly strong assumptions (cf. section
8.1
) are required to conclude that
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
can be determined from the observed data, the assumptions that would be required to determine (even “approximately” or “on average”)
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
seem to be hopelessly strong. Considering how the strata of the analogous estimand that does not ignore the multiple representatives problem would be defined in terms of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
, this seems to be an insurmountable problem for (directly) estimating such an estimand.

One might try to sidestep this limitation by screening for “suspicious” outliers in the identified sample that might correspond to values of
N^{(s_{1})}(0)
(or
N^{(\sigma)}(0)
for
\sigma\not=s_{1},s_{2}
) other than
1
. This would be analogous and closely related to the hypothetical strategy discussed at length in the comments at the end of section
8.1.2
, for how to possibly weaken the “no censoring” assumption. Thus this strategy would suffer from the same problems, namely requiring a very strong “signal to noise ratio”, and being very difficult to implement successfully even given a strong “signal to noise ratio”. Thus it is left to future work to implement and validate such a strategy, if it is even possible.

4
Observed Data Model

The observed data model
\mathcal{M}^{O}
corresponds to the likelihoods from equations (
7.6
), (
7.9
), and (
7.13
). The map
\phi^{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}^{F}\to\mathcal{M}^{O}
sending distributions in the full data model to distributions in the observed data model marginalizes over all of the unobserved time points. Recall from section
4
that this means all cell counts at all times are censored, except for the final cell counts. The difference between the full data model and the observed data model that is salient for the definitions of the ATE estimands is that the observed data model assumes that
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
/
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
is unknown and that only
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t)
is known.

5
Observed Data ATE Definitions,

Identifiability

What has so far been glibly ignored is the obvious issue that the droplets are observed at only one time point. In particular, the droplets are not observed upon formation at
t=0
. There is no way to observe
N^{(s)}_{d}(0)
neither for any strain
s
nor for any droplet
d
. Without further assumptions, our targeted estimands, defined in terms of the full data in section
3
above, cannot possibly be defined in terms of the observed data alone.

By omitting expressions not belonging to the observed data in the definitions from section
3
of the full data ATE estimands, we arrive at the following definitions for observed data ATE estimands. These estimands, rather than “normalizing” by the (unobservable) initial counts of cells to get a proper “growth factor”, instead consider only the final counts of cells. Moreover, instead of defining the treatment and control groups in terms of the unobservable initial presence/absence of strains, these estimands define the treatment and control groups in terms of the observable presence/absence of strains at time
t
.

(Observed Data) Picky treatment, picky control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi_{p/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{O}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi_{p/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{O}&\mapsto&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\end{array}

(5.1)

(Observed Data) Picky treatment, gluttonous control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi_{p/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{O}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi_{p/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{O}&\mapsto&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\supseteq\{s_{2}\},s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\right]\end{array}

(5.2)

(Observed Data)
Gluttonous treatment, picky control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi_{g/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{O}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi_{g/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{O}&\mapsto&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\end{array}

(5.3)

(Observed Data)
Gluttonous treatment, gluttonous control:

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi_{g/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{O}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi_{g/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{O}&\mapsto&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&&&-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\supseteq\{s_{2}\},s_{1}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\right]\end{array}

(5.4)

Under certain assumptions (that are satisfied e.g. by the hPoMu and hNBDM working models), the full data ATE estimands (which assume
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
is known) would equal the observed data ATE estimands. This means the full data ATE estimands would be identifiable from the observed data. The assumptions are:

•

we always have that
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))
,

•

the marginal distributions of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
, i.e. the distributions of
N^{(1)}(0)
,
N^{(2)}(0)
, …,
N^{(S)}(0)
, are all mutually independent.

Cf. section
8
and figure
7.6
below.

Figure 7.6
:
\mathcal{M}^{F}
is the full statistical model,
\mathcal{M}^{O}
is the observed statistical model (i.e. corresponding to the observed data and observed likelihoods),
\phi^{\mathcal{M}}
sends each distribution in the full statistical model to the corresponding distribution of observed data,
\psi^{F}
is the estimand mapping defined on the full statistical model answering the scientific question we are interested in. This estimand is called identifiable if there exists a corresponding estimand defined on the observed statistical model such that the above commutative diagram commutes. (For an introduction to commutative diagrams, cf. e.g.
[
LS09
]
.)

Another way to interpret the observed data ATE estimands is as pretending that
N^{(s)}(0)=1
always (whenever it is nonzero). This may not be entirely unreasonable. The experiment can be set up so that
1
is the highest probability nonzero count of cells belonging to any given strain at a droplet’s formation. (Cf. the discussion in section
5.C
.) Hence, even when we do not believe the assumptions made in section
8
to be valid, the experiment can still be calibrated to minimize the “identification gap” between the full data ATE estimands and the observed data ATE estimands, which makes the estimands useful.

5.1
Stratified Observed Data Average Treatment Effect

The motivation for using the following definition is explained in section
7
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rccl}\Psi(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{M}^{O}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
\Psi(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):&\mathcal{P}_{O}&\mapsto&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(t)=1,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)\right]\right.\\
&&&\left.\hphantom{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\right]}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(t)=0,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)\right]\right]\\
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\mathbf{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{S-2}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(t)=1,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right]\right.\\
&&&\displaystyle\left.-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{O}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(t)=0,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right]\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}_{F}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right)\,.\end{array}

(5.5)

Like definition (
3.5
), this definition also implicitly uses a definition of treatment and covariates (cf. section
7
) that ignores the multiple representatives problem (cf. section
5.C
). For a discussion of many of the issues that would be entailed by trying to use an estimand analogous to (
5.5
) that addresses the multiple representatives problem, cf. section
3.1
.

\Psi

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
. The latter are defined explicitly below.

(Estimator)
Picky treatment, picky control:

\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{p/p}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):=&\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\
&-\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\\
\end{split}

(6.1)

(Estimator)
Picky treatment, gluttonous control:

\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}_{p/g}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):=&\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right]\\

(6.2)

(Estimator)

6.1
Stratified Estimator Definition and TMLE

The motivation for using the following definition is explained in section
7
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})&:=&\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\mathbf{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{S-2}}\left[\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(t)=1,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right]\right.\\
&&\displaystyle\left.-\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(t)=0,\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right]\right]\cdot\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{d\in[{D}]}\mathbb{I}\!\left\{\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}_{d}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(t)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}\right\}}{{D}}\,.\end{array}

(6.5)

Note that we can do much better than this naïve plugin estimator when it comes to estimating a stratified ATE estimand of the form (
3.5
) or (
5.5
). Even when it is not possible in finite samples to stratify over all possible values of the covariates (cf. sections
7.1
and
7.3
), for our initial estimate it is still possible to use SuperLearner (see
[
vdLPH07
]
and
[
vdLR11
, Chapter 3]
) to implement an ensemble machine learning approach that does not require us to make additional assumptions that we do not actually believe. For example, we could use different settings for GLMnet
[
FHT10
]
regression on the binary treatment and covariates as some of the learners in the ensemble considered by SuperLearner.

Afterwards, we can use the targeting step as implemented in e.g. the
R
package ’
tmle
’
[
GL12
]
for targeted minimum-loss based estimation (TMLE). This adjusts for the high-dimensional covariates in a way that makes the initial estimate produced by SuperLearner unbiased. Targeted learning leads to the most efficient and effective use of the available data possible, because our efforts are focused only on estimating the portion
\mathcal{Q}_{O}
of the observed probability distribution
\mathcal{P}_{O}
that is relevant for determining our chosen target parameter (
5.5
). The targeted learning story has already been developed in detail for this kind of estimand, cf.
[
vdLR11
]
and
[
vdLR18
]
. The first five chapters of
[
vdLR11
]
are particularly relevant. In this preliminary exposition I do not go into the full details of implementing the targeted learning roadmap for this class of problem. However that is the goal of future work.

7
Confounder Adjustment and Positivity

Although not mentioned explicitly in sections
8.4
and
2.1
, the gluttonous and picky groupings are two strategies for adjusting for possible confounders. These two strategies lie at opposite ends of a tradeoff. The tradeoff is between, on the one hand, using all droplets that might possibly be informative for inferring the effect of one strain on the other, and, on the other hand, completely controlling for the presence of third strains. To not give up on being able to use all possibly informative droplets, the gluttonous groups give up on being able to completely control for the presence of third strains. In order to not give up on being able to completely control for the presence of third strains, the picky groups give up on being able to use all possibly informative droplets.

As discussed in section
3
, one reason for choosing to analyze data from MOREI, rather than from e.g. an observational metagenome study, is that the encapsulation in droplets should already control for confounding due to other strains
\sigma\not=s_{1},s_{2}
as much as possible. In particular, one of the goals is so that the data is as comparable as possible to the “gold standard” of manually plating cell cultures (cf. e.g.
[
VCF+18
]
), for which
6
6

6

Here
\vec{\mathbf{0}}_{S-2}
is used to denote the all-
0
’s vector of length
(S-2)
.

\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{0}}_{S-2}
always. Our goal is that, all other things being equal
7
7

7

E.g. the experiment is set up reasonably so that the expected number of cells per droplet
\lambda
is not too large, in particular not a number much larger than
2
. Cf. again section
4.1
.
, in general
\vec{\mathbf{0}}_{S-2}
will be the value of
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)
that occurs with the highest probability. As long as we have enough data to make a reliable, estimate using the picky groups only, that is what we always prefer to do. Whenever we use any droplets for which
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)\not=\vec{\mathbf{0}}_{S-2}
while making estimates, it is (almost)
always
due to necessity induced by “data starvation” (cf. again the discussion in section
8.4
). This last point is important to keep in mind for later.

7.1
Outcomes, Treatments, Covariates

The problem of confounder adjustment or control can be understood within the framework of splitting the observed data (“
O
”) between (i) outcomes/effects (“
Y
”), (ii) treatments/exposures (“
A
”), and (iii) covariates/potential confounders (“
W
”) as is done e.g. in the references
[
vdLR11
]
,
[
vdLR18
]
, and
[
PPG+10
]
. Recall that throughout this chapter we are using ATEs to study the effect of strain
s_{1}
on the growth of strain
s_{2}
.

The definitions in section
5
correctly suggest that the outcome
Y
we are interested in is
\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)
. When we assume the full data is available and known, then the definitions in section
3
correctly suggest that the outcome
Y
we are interested in becomes
\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)
.

The treatment
A
we are interested in is the presence/absence
\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)
of strain
s_{1}
. Observe how, by using the binary
\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)
as the treatment instead of
N^{(s_{1})}(0)
, we are effectively choosing to ignore the multiple representatives problem (cf. section
5.C
). It is clear that the value of
\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)
is the treatment variable, because (given a fixed choice between “gluttonous” or “picky”) a value of
\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)=1
, which is equivalent to the condition
\{s_{1}\}\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))
, is always what distinguishes the treatment group from the control group, the latter corresponding to
\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)=0
or equivalently
\{s_{1}\}\not\in\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))
.

Finally this leaves us to address the covariates, or potential confounders,
W
. These correspond of course to strains
\sigma\not=s_{1},s_{2}
which are not directly relevant to the effect of strain
s_{1}
on the growth of
s_{2}
(although of course they might potentially mediate that effect, cf. e.g. section
2
,
[
MXS17
]
, or
[
BKK16
]
). With the length
S
binary random vector
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
representing the presence/absence of all
S
strains at time
0
, let
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)
denote the length
(S-2)
binary random vector containing the
(S-2)
entries of
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
corresponding to the
S-2
strains
\sigma\not=s_{1},s_{2}
, i.e. all indices in
\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}:=[S]\setminus\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
. Reviewing the definitions of (
3.5
), (
5.5
), and (
6.5
), and because the definitions of the gluttonous groups differ from those of their picky counterparts only
8
8

8

I.e. irrespective of the particular values of the nonzero entries of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
.

in the presence/absence of strains
\sigma\not=s_{1},s_{2}
, it is hopefully clear that the relevant choice of covariates, and thus potential confounders, is
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)
. Observe how considering entries of the vector
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
, rather than entries of the vector
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
, in defining our covariates
W
again implies that we are choosing to ignore the multiple representatives problem (cf. section
5.C
). Notice also that the picky definitions amount to requiring that we only consider droplets for which the value of
all
entries of
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)
is
0
, whereas the gluttonous definitions allow us to consider droplets for which
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)
has
any
of its
2^{S-2}
possible values in
\{0,1\}^{S-2}
.

7.2
Stratify to Attempt to Escape Tradeoff

To avoid the tradeoff for which the picky groups and gluttonous groups are positioned at opposite ends, we might like to be able to use all possibly informative droplets while still controlling for the presence of strains
\sigma\not=s_{1},s_{2}

in some way
, even if not completely controlling for them by excluding all droplets where they are present. We can achieve such a compromise by stratifying over the effects that occur for all possible values
\vec{\mathbf{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{S-2}
of
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)
and weighting the effects according to the probabilities for
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{b}}
. Cf. the definitions (
3.5
), (
5.5
), and (
6.5
). Such an approach in fact corresponds to the standard definition of average treatment effect (cf. chapters
1
and
2
of
[
vdLR11
]
).

Observe how the conditional expectation estimands for strata
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
implying the presence of
3
or more strains in the definitions (
3.5
) and (
5.5
) amount to quantifications of the effects of “higher-order interactions”, cf. section
2
. Thus the stratifications in the definitions (
3.5
), (
5.5
), and (
6.5
) can legitimately be interpreted as “stratifying over all relevant higher-order interactions”. Hence being able to use (
6.5
) to effectively estimate (
3.5
) or (
5.5
) requires being able to effectively estimate all higher-order interactions, which as discussed before in sections
8.5
and
2
is where we begin to encounter serious problems.

In particular, the usefulness of the stratified ATE requires that the experimental treatment assignment (ETA) assumption, also known as the positivity assumption, be satisfied
[
vdLR11
, p. 35]
. Quoting from
[
PPG+10
]
:

Positivity violations can arise for two reasons. First, it may be theoretically impossible for individuals with certain covariate values to receive a given exposure of interest… Second, violations or near violations of positivity can arise in finite samples due to chance.

The first kind of violation, the theoretical impossibility, corresponds to some of the conditional expectation estimands that are summands in the expression (
3.5
) not being well-defined. The second kind of violation or near violation, occurring for finite samples, can correspond to some of the plugin estimators for conditional expectations that are summands in the expression (
6.5
) either not being well-defined in the finite sample
9
9

9

With
0
observations in the finite sample of at least one of the treatment or control group for that stratum, the corresponding sample conditional mean(s) would not be well-defined.

or being unreliable/having high variance in the finite sample
10
10

10

Due to the number of observations in the finite sample of at least one of the treatment or control group for that stratum being very small, even if the number of observations for both is technically nonzero. The corresponding sample conditional mean(s) would be unreliable.

. Although the first kind of violation is not relevant here, I argue in the next section that in practice it is impossible for us to avoid the second kind of violation or near violation of the positivity assumption.

7.3
Why Positivity Violations are Likely Unavoidable in Practice, if not in Principle

To emphasize, the following bounds are not meant to be precise consequences proven from certain fixed assumptions, they are intended to be entirely heuristic. The basic ideas are that (1) the probability of a given strain appearing in a droplet should be roughly proportional to its relative abundance in the sampling population, and that (2) the probabilities of multiple strains appearing in the same droplet should be (“to
0
th or
1
st order”) roughly multiplicative.

The number of treatment samples for the effect of strain
s_{1}
on the growth of strain
s_{2}
can be thought of as roughly
11
11

11

E.g. assuming all strains have approximately the same relative abundance
\frac{1}{S}
.
proportional to
\frac{1}{S^{2}}
or
12
12

12

Strictly speaking this should be considered a blatant misuse of Landau Big-Oh notation.

O(\frac{1}{S^{2}})
in probability, whereas the number of control samples for the effect of strain
s_{1}
on the growth of
s_{2}
can be thought of as roughly proportional to
\frac{1}{S}
or
O(\frac{1}{S})
in probability. Cf. section
2.C
for essentially the same idea.

In particular, the number of samples that contain at least one of each of
I
given strains
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
, …,
s_{I}
would be roughly proportional to
\frac{1}{S^{I}}
or
O(\frac{1}{S^{I}})
in probability. Thus, even as the number of strata
\vec{\mathbf{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{S-2}
in the definitions (
3.5
), (
5.5
), and (
6.5
)
increases
exponentially with increasing
S
, at the same time the number of samples available for most strata
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
roughly
decreases
exponentially (in probability) with increasing
S
. Thus, even in the absence of any theoretical positivity violations, i.e. even if
\mathbb{P}(\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{b}})>0
for all strata
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
, in practice we are almost always
13
13

13

It should probably go without saying, but this is because it is not physically possible to indefinitely increase the total number of
{D}
exponentially with respect to increasing
S
.

confronted with massive finite sample positivity violations. This is especially the case in the regime where
S
is large, which is the regime of greatest scientific novelty and thus interest. Cf. the similar problem motivating
[
TRKS+17
]
.

Cf. also section
8.5
, which discusses a closely related issue. Note again that conditional expectation estimands for strata
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
implying the presence of
3
or more strains effectively correspond to quantifying the effects of “higher-order interactions”. While not explicitly targeting these higher-order interactions may possibly limit the usefulness of ecological interpretations of any results (cf. e.g.
[
BKK16
]
or
[
MXS17
]
for arguments to this effect), as discussed before already in section
2
such a point is irrelevant to the extent that we simply lack the data required to reliably estimate these effects. (All of this also ignores the inevitable computational/algorithmic difficulties associated with attempting to estimate exponentially many parameters, as was mentioned before in section
8.5
.)

7.4
Chosen Approaches to Positivity Violations

Section 6.5 of
[
PPG+10
]
describes any strategy for approaching positivity violations as a “tradeoff” between “altering the target parameter” and “improved identifiability”. Earlier I described both the picky and gluttonous approaches as opposite ends of another tradeoff. I explain below how the tradeoff mentioned earlier can be considered a special case of the tradeoff described in
[
PPG+10
]
.

In particular, the picky approach is a special case of the “trimming” strategy described in section 6.3 of
[
PPG+10
]
. By restricting the sample to only include the stratum for which
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{0}}_{S-2}
, we improve identifiability by removing the possibility of confounding due to the presence of strains
\sigma\not=s_{1},s_{2}
.

The gluttonous approach does not seem to correspond directly to any of the strategies mentioned explicitly in
[
PPG+10
]
or chapter 8 of
[
vdLR11
]
, although it may be analogous to the “projection” approach mentioned therein for marginal structural models. The idea of the gluttonous approach is to alter the target parameter by “collapsing all of the strata into a single stratum”. Because there is now only a single stratum, there is no longer any need to restrict samples according to their stratum membership, which in some sense improves the finite-sample identifiability from observed data. This is, of course, at the cost of substantially altering the research question being asked.

This leads to the question of how one chooses between the two approaches in practice. As implied above, in the introduction to
7
, and sections
8.4
and
2.1
, the main criterion is “data starvation”. While it is true in general that the stratum corresponding to
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}^{\{s_{1},s_{2}\}^{c}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{0}}_{S-2}
is usually the stratum with the highest probability of occurrence, especially for smaller finite samples this is often still not enough to guarantee that both sample conditional means in definition (
6.1
) are even well-defined
14
14

14

Much less calculated from a large enough sample to have manageable variance.
. Thus the “trimming” strategy only works when we have sufficient data from our largest stratum. When we lack sufficient data from our largest stratum, then it is perhaps unsurprising that our only remaining choice is to collapse all strata, i.e. adopt the gluttonous approach. Even then that sometimes is still not enough.

Recall from earlier (e.g. sections
2.C
and
7.3
) how in general we are much more likely to suffer “data starvation” for the treatment groups than for the control groups. With that in mind, preliminary results (not shown) from simulated data appear to support the “data starvation” criterion for choosing between the picky and gluttonous approaches. In particular, for combinations involving strains with smaller relative abundances (
\approx 2\%
or less) and thus having smaller finite-sample sizes, the “gluttonous treatment and gluttonous control” approach, cf. equation (
6.4
), appeared to be the most effective. Due to the small sample sizes, “data starvation” was evidently occurring for both the treatment and control groups. However, for combinations involving strains with larger relative abundances (
\approx 5-10\%
) the “gluttonous treatment and picky control” approach appeared to be the most effective, cf. equation (
6.3
). Due to the slightly larger sample sizes, “data starvation” evidently was not a factor for the control groups, allowing the picky approach’s stricter controlling for confounding to reap benefits. However, because treatment group sizes are much smaller than control group sizes, even given the larger sample sizes the treatment group sizes were still sufficiently small that the correction for “data starvation” implemented by the gluttonous approach remained useful. These preliminary results were not only for average treatment effect estimators, but also for other kinds of estimands (e.g. conditional correlations or rank-based coefficients). That the same pattern occurred for all of these different kinds of estimands strongly suggests that their shared tradeoff, between available sample size and strictness of controlling for confounders, most plausibly explains the pattern.

One might object to the collapsing of all strata implicit in the gluttonous approach as being too odious. However, in choosing to ignore the multiple representatives problem (cf. section
5.C
) by using binary variables
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
as our exposure and covariates, rather than the count variables
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
, we were also effectively already choosing to collapse strata. Cf. again section
7.1
. For example, a stratum corresponding to
\mathbb{S}^{(s_{1})}(0)=1
can be considered collapsing strata corresponding to
N^{(s_{1})}(0)=1
,
N^{(s_{1})}(0)=2
,
N^{(s_{1})}(0)=3
, …. Therefore I argue that the gluttonous approach should not be considered any more odious than our previous choice to ignore the multiple representatives problem.

It is true that there is at least one important difference. Collapsing the strata corresponding to
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
is necessitated by a lack of identifiability that in principle may hold for arbitrary, even infinite, sample sizes. In contrast, the further collapse of strata employed by the gluttonous approach is only due to finite-sample constraints. However, as argued before in section
7.3
, given that the sample sizes necessary to overcome those finite-sample constraints would be exponential, and thus usually not even physically possible, I argue that in practice this difference amounts to an unimportant distinction. Thus even if the gluttonous approach is odious, I argue that in practical terms it is no less of a necessary evil than choosing to ignore the multiple representatives problem.

8
Sufficient Conditions for Identifiability

Section
8.1
in effect addresses the question: why (given that we may be able to ignore the multiple representatives problem, cf. section
5.C
) is the presence/absence of strains at the final time
t
sufficient to completely characterize the presence/absence of strains initially at time
0
? Section
8.2
in effect addresses the question: why can we ignore the multiple representatives problem?

Comment:

Recall from section
2.1.1
how
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0))
. Yet at the same time
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))
if and only if
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)=\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(t)
. Hence claims about the identifiability of
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))
can equally be interpreted as claims about the identifiability of
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
. In particular, even in the presence of an identification gap between the full data estimand
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
and the observed data estimand
\Psi(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
, because the assumptions for identifiability of
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))
from the observed data are (arguably) much milder, it is still appealing to define treatment and control groups in terms of
\vec{\mathbf{\mathbb{S}}}(0)
instead of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
and thereby neglect the multiple representatives problem (cf. section
5.C
).

8.1
Determination of
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))
from Observed Data

The assumption that

\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\,,

(8.1)

can be broken down into two assumptions.

8.1.1
No Spontaneous Generation

This assumption says that the number of strains within any given droplet can not increase over time, throughout the course of the experiment:

\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\supseteq\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\,.

(8.2)

In other words, strains that were not present during the droplet’s formation can not later spontaneously appear within the droplet.

This is certainly true if we consider only the incubation of the droplets (cf. section
4.2
). As Louis Pasteur already demonstrated in the 19th century using a swan neck flask, the creation of cells of a given strain of microbe requires the prior presence of cells of that strain. The theory of “spontaneous generation” is false, so microbes of a given strain can not spontaneously arise from non-living matter, preventing the number of strains from spontaneously increasing. Thus prima facie this assumption appears to be entirely unquestionable.

However, the name of the assumption is somewhat misleading. In practice, the implausible occurrence of spontaneous generation may not actually be necessary for this assumption to be violated. Indeed, experimental error that causes some small percentage of the droplets to be merged incorrectly, or to otherwise incorrectly receive the same barcode (cf. sections
4.3
and
2
) would cause a similar effect to this assumption being violated. Preliminary results suggested that this effect may be negligible for inference. The simulation found little to no deleterious effect on inference caused by the rate of merging error assumed by the simulation. My collaborator also said that MOREI protocol has subsequently been improved to drastically further reduce the rate of merging error. Hence, although this assumption may not be
entirely
unquestionable in practice, it still appears to be a safe assumption to make.

8.1.2
No Censoring

This corresponds to the other direction, namely the assumption that the number of strains cannot decrease over time, throughout the course of the experiment:

\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\,.

(8.3)

In other words, once a given strain is present in a droplet, it can never disappear. This assumption is actually much less realistic in practice than that from section
8.1.1
. In particular, in the case of antagonistic interactions, where one strain harms another, we would expect the antagonized strain to quite often go extinct.

Thus the failure of this identifiability assumption poses particular danger to the inference of antagonistic microbial interactions (negative edge weights in the signed network representation, cf. section
2
). However, as discussed in sections
4.3
,
2
, and
3
, given that the data we observe does not actually correspond exactly to the counts of living cells
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t)
at the time of sequencing
t
, there are reasons to believe that such censoring concerns could be mitigated by phenomena that occur in practice
15
15

15

Preliminary results suggest that, while avoiding censoring this way could increase our ability to infer antagonistic interactions, it could also decrease our ability to infer the magnitude of beneficial interactions (which correspond to positive edges in the signed network representation, cf. again section
2
). This is because overcounting the number of living benefit-inducing cells required to induce a certain growth advantage (by including “relic” genetic material in the count) causes us to underestimate the strength of the beneficial growth effect. Therefore “relic” genetic material is potentially a double-edged sword.

, e.g. ‘‘relic’’ genetic material
16
16

16

Cf. section
4.3
and appendix
2
for more discussion of “relic” genetic material.

. In other words, we might expect the sequencing results to be more roughly proportional to the total number of cells that have lived
ever
, rather than necessarily be roughly proportional to the current number of living cells. Cf. section
2
and figure
A.2.11
in particular.

Whether censoring occurs depends not only on the living or dying of cells during the second (incubation) phase of the experiment as described in section
4
, but also on possible effects of PCR amplification that occurs during the third (sequencing) phase. This presents several possible complications. One is the possible “swamping out” in the sequencing results of the signal from strains with small relative abundances. Cf. footnote
14
from section
4.3
. Regardless of whether the genetic material is “relic” or from living cells, if the proportion corresponding tor a given strain is small, then its sequence might not show up in the sequencing results after PCR amplification.
Cf. the discussion of “detection limits” as a possible cause of “zero inflation” in
[
MGSD19
, section 1.1]
.
Thus a strain might not be registered as having been in the droplet even though the droplet contained genetic material from living and/or dead cells of the strain. (Such effects could be called “technical zeros” using the terminology of
[
PGB21
]
.) This is related to “jackpot effects”, cf. e.g.
[
MSH+19
]
.

In particular, the number of reads after PCR amplification is not necessarily a linear function of the initial number of reads, not even “on average”. In the case of such “non-linear PCR amplification”, the relative proportions of reads for each of the strains before PCR amplification may not still be the same after PCR amplification. This is in contrast to the predictions of the simple, naïve model mentioned in appendix
3
, for which the number of amplified reads is a constant linear factor times some multiplicative noise. Besides potential non-linearity or multiplicative noise of PCR amplification, a further factor that might exacerbate “technical zeros” resulting from “swamping out” are sequence-specific PCR amplification biases. See
[
MWC19
]
or appendix
2
for a discussion.

Ultimately though, as stated before at the beginning of chapter
2
:

The problem of choosing good statistical models of PCR amplification is applicable to an extremely wide range of biological experiments. It should be its own project and fully addressing it is outside of the scope of this work. Previous work that has more substantially considered similar problems includes
[
YRCS06
]

[
KMP+07
]

[
YWSJ08
]

[
DH15
]
[
MWC19
]
[
MSH+19
]
.

Future research directions could include (1) gauging the extent to which experimental artifacts actually do blunt the effects of censoring in practice, (2) developing methods to adjust the analysis to account for the possibility of censoring, (3) gauging how often censoring might occur in practice, or (4) gauging to what extent failure of the no censoring assumption might bias the results (i.e. the size of the identification gap) in practice.

Comments:

This assumption also appears to effectively be necessary to sidestep the issue of whether it makes sense to use the convention that “
\log(0)=0
”. Namely because any droplets for which
N^{(s_{2})}(t)=0
are always de facto excluded from the sample of droplets for which we assume that
N^{(s_{2})}(0)\geq 1
. This is because the no censoring assumption implies that such droplets do not exist, and because de facto we have no way to reliably identify them anyway, given that our only observations of the droplets are at time
t
, and not time
0
.
Any future work which claimed to be able to identify such droplets would then have to put more thought into what convention should be used for “
\log(0)
”.
Such “zero inflation” problems however are not inherently unique to the analysis of MOREI-like data, cf. for example
[
MGSD19
]
or
[
PGB21
]
. (However the problems posed by zeros are in many ways different for the compositional datasets studied in
[
MGSD19
]
or
[
PGB21
]
, whereas the data from MOREI is inherently not compositional, cf. again the discussion of spikein genes from section
4.3
. Cf. e.g.
[
CHL+20
]
or
[
SWMD17
]
for subtleties of using compositional data.)

One might try to circumvent the limitations of this assumption as follows, at least for censoring caused by antagonistic interactions. Namely, given an appropriate “neutral model” predicting which percentage of droplets should contain certain combinations of strains based on the strains’ relative abundances, we could look at whether any combinations have “suspiciously” small numbers of observations. From that we might conclude that in fact some of the droplets containing that combination at time
0
were not counted due to one of the relevant strains becoming extinguished by time
t
. For example, if strain
s_{1}
harms strain
s_{2}
, and we see “suspiciously” few droplets containing both
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, we might conclude that several of the droplets seeming to only contain strain
s_{1}
most likely originally also contained strain
s_{2}
but that subsequently strain
s_{2}
became extinct in those droplets by time
t
.

Even ignoring the obvious (and potentially insurmountable) difficulty of identifying which droplets seeming to contain only
s_{1}
actually originally also contained strain
s_{2}
at time
0
, there are at least two issues with such a proposal.

First, the expected number of droplets containing a given combination of strains might be very small and thus subject to large variance (or at least large “noise to signal ratio”, cf. the discussion of a similar issue in footnote
14
from section
4.3
). For example, even in a toy neutral model where the observed count of any given combination of strains varies usually by no more than
\pm 2
from its expected count, if the expected count is
5
and the observed count is
3
or
2
, there is very little “signal” available with which to distinguish the two possibilities of random variation and censoring due to antagonistic interaction. Cf. the related discussion about practical violations of the positivity assumption in section
7
. This is in contrast to e.g. a situation where the expected count is
50
and the observed count is
20
(instead of e.g.
48
or
47
).

Second, even if we somehow manage to create a large enough total number of droplets
{D}
such that all of the combinations of strains have large enough expected counts to have amenable “signal to noise ratios”, we are still left with the problem of identifying an appropriate neutral model to compare against in order to flag “suspicious” observed counts. Given previous work using the Poisson distribution as the default neutral model for total cell counts in droplets for single-cell sequencing experiments (e.g.
[
CNd+15
]
), it is tempting to accept the hPoMu working model as “the unquestionably appropriate neutral working model”. However, even retaining the assumption that the total number of cells in each droplet is Poisson distributed (e.g. as is also the case for the hPoDM working model), one of the major conclusions of chapter
5
is that in a neutral model the observed counts can still be “statistically significantly” lower than the expected counts predicted by hPoMu. Preliminary work (not shown) also suggested that trying to identify “suspicious” low observed counts using hPoMu as a neutral model is most likely doomed to failure, with almost all examples identified as potential instances of censoring due to antagonism being false positives caused by the bias introduced by the misspecified choice of neutral model.

Thus choosing an appropriate neutral model requires either fitting to some relevant data, or establishing a substantial body of research that identifies what is in most scenarios an appropriate neutral model (assuming that such an appropriate “universal” choice even exists, which it may not). One reason for recommending the “
t=0
pre-experiment” described in section
8
is because, by not incubating the droplets, it allows us to control for the possibility that “suspicious” observed counts are due to antagonistic interactions, rather than an incorrectly chosen neutral model. Therefore the “
t=0
pre-experiment” is ideally suited for producing data with which to fit an optimal neutral model.

8.2
Identifiability of
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
from Observed Data

With respect to the notation used in the following lemmas below, one has that the picky treatment ATE estimands correspond to the choice:

\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}:=\left\{\Omega\subseteq[S]:\Omega=\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right\}\,,

(8.4)

while the gluttonous treatment ATE estimands correspond to the choice:

\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}:=\left\{\Omega\subseteq[S]:\Omega\supseteq\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\right\}\,.

(8.5)

Similarly, the picky control ATE estimands correspond to the choice:

\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}:=\left\{\Omega\subseteq[S]:\Omega=\{s_{2}\}\right\}\,,

(8.6)

and the gluttonous control ATE estimands correspond to the choice:

\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}:=\left\{\Omega\subseteq[S]:\Omega\supseteq\{s_{2}\},s_{1}\not\in\Omega\right\}\,.

(8.7)

Lemma 7.1
.

Assuming that

•

the marginal distributions of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
are mutually independent, e.g. as turns out to be true for the hPoMu and hNBDM distributions,

•

that the distributions of the droplets are independent,

•

we always have that
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))
(cf. section
8.1
),

then for any two subsets
\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\left([S]\right)
of the power set of
[S]
such that
s_{2}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
for all
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
s_{2}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
for all
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}
, then the estimator

\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):=&\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]\\
&-\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(N^{(s_{2})}(t)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\right]\end{split}

(8.8)

is statistically consistent for the full data estimand assuming that
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
is known

\begin{split}\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2}):=&\mathbb{E}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]\\
&-\mathbb{E}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\right]\,.\end{split}

(8.9)

Proof of Lemma
7.1
:

Proof of identifiability of
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
from the observed data:

Using the properties of logarithms and the linearity of expectation one has that the expression (
8.9
) defining
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
above equals

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(t))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(t))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\right]\\
-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(0))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(0))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\right]\right)\,.\end{split}

(8.10)

By Lemma
7.2
using
\phi(n)=\log(n)
, the expression inside of parentheses in (
8.10
) vanishes. Thus in this case
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})=\Psi(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})&=&\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(t))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(t))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\right]\\
&=&\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(t))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(t))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\right]\\
&=&\Psi(t)(s_{1},s_{2})\,,\end{array}

(8.11)

where we used the assumption that
\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))=\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))
always.

Proof of consistency of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
for
\Psi(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
:

Apply the Weak Law of Large Numbers to the first terms of (
8.8
) and (
8.9
) and the second terms of (
8.8
) and (
8.9
) respectively, and then use the fact
\mathbf{X}_{n}\overset{P}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{n}\overset{P}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{Y}
implies
(\mathbf{X}_{n}-\mathbf{Y}_{n})\overset{P}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y}
.
∎

8.2.1
Practical Limitations of Identifiability Result

Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{F}
denote the “oracle plugin estimator” for
\Psi^{F}(t)(s_{1},s_{2})
. (This can not be computed from the observed data, but an “all-knowing oracle” knows the observed data and the unobserved data from time
0
, and thus can compute plugin estimators that are functions of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
.) By the law of large numbers and the continuous mapping theorem we have that the oracle plugin estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{F}
converges in probability to
\Psi^{F}
, basically for the same reason that the observed data plugin estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
converges in probability to
\Psi
.

Now under the assumptions of Lemma
7.1
we have that
both
the oracle plugin estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{F}
and the observed data plugin estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}
converge in probability to the
\Psi^{F}
(because
\Psi^{F}=\Psi
, which again is not true in general). However, this does
not
mean that we also have that the oracle plugin estimator equals the observed data plugin estimator. Indeed, although
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{F}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}})
approaches
0
in probability, it is still the case that
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{F}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}})\not=0
. In particular, for finite samples the difference between the oracle and observed data plugin estimators will usually not be zero
17
17

17

This is related to the multiple representatives problem that is described in section
5.C
. Figure
5.C.1
illustrates clearly why the oracle and observed data plugin estimators can differ.
, and it may not even converge
quickly
to
0
in probability.

Thus the usefulness of the result of Lemma
7.1
in practice depends on how quickly the random variable
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{F}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}})
converges to zero in probability. (We would like a finite-sample “with high probability” bound, but an asymptotic convergence rate in probability would not be bad either.)
Because the size of the treatment group
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
sample will typically grow much more slowly than the size of the control group
\mathbf{C}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
sample, the rate at which difference between the oracle and observed data plugin estimators converges to
0
in probability should probably be bounded by the rate at which the difference
18
18

18

Note that the quantity
\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]
is also an “oracle estimator” that cannot be computed from the observed data. In fact it is the first term of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Psi}}^{F}
.

\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log\left(\frac{N^{(s_{2})}(t)}{N^{(s_{2})}(0)}\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]-\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log(N^{(s_{2})}(t))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(t))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]
converges to
0
in probability.
The latter difference only approaches zero in probability as the size of the treatment group sample grows
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
without bound.
Hence to the extent that
\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},s_{2})}
grows slowly in probability, we should not necessarily expect the difference between the oracle and observed data plugin estimators to converge quickly to
0
in probability.

8.2.2
Proofs of Supporting Lemmas

Lemma 7.2
.

Assuming that

•

the marginal distributions of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
are mutually independent, e.g. as turns out to be true for the hPoMu and hNBDM distributions,

•

that
\mathscr{O}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\left([S]\right)
is such that
s_{2}\in\Omega
for all
\Omega\in\mathscr{O}
,

then for any measurable
\phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
one has

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s_{2})}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s_{2})}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\,.

(8.12)

In particular, for any two subsets
\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\left([S]\right)
of the power set of
[S]
such that
s_{2}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
for all
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
s_{2}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
for all
\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}
one has that

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s_{2})}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{T}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s_{2})}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{C}}\right]\,,

(8.13)

I.e. the value does not depend on the particular subset of the power set, as long as that subset of the power set is such that
s_{2}
is in every set belonging to it.

It may be unnecessary to assume that
\phi
is non-negative. I want to preclude potential pathological situations where e.g. the infinite summation is convergent but not absolutely convergent, and ensure that tools like the monotone convergence theorem are available.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
:
Using the law of total expectation, one has that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s_{2})}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}\right]\\
=&\sum_{\Omega\in\mathscr{O}}\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(N^{(s_{2})}(0))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\right]\mathbb{P}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}\right)\\
=&\sum_{\Omega\in\mathscr{O}}\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(N^{(s_{2})}(0))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}\right)\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(N^{(s_{2})}(0))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\cdot\left(\sum_{\Omega\in\mathscr{O}}\mathbb{P}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))\in\mathscr{O}\right)\right)\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s_{2})}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s_{2}\}\right]\cdot(1)\,,\end{split}

(8.14)

where the second equality follows from Lemma
7.3
, the third equality is the distributive property, and the last equality follows because the events are disjoint and exhaustive, thus their probabilities sum to
1
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Assuming that

•

the marginal distributions of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
are mutually independent, e.g. as turns out to be true for the hPoMu and hNBDM distributions,

then for any
\Omega\subseteq[S]
such that
s\in\Omega
one has for measurable
\phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s)}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s)}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s\}\right]\,.

(8.15)

Proof of Lemma
7.3
:
Applying the law of total expectation, one has

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s)}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\right]\\
=&\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s)}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)=n\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)=n\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\right)\\
=&\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s)}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)=n\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)=n\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s\}\right)\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(N^{(s)}(0)\right)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s\}\right]\,,\end{split}

(8.16)

where the second equality was an application of Lemma
7.4
, and the last equality another use of the law of total expectation.
\square

Lemma 7.4
.

Assuming that

•

the marginal distributions of
\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0)
are mutually independent, e.g. as turns out to be true for the hPoMu and hNBDM distributions,

then for any
\Omega\subseteq[S]
such that
s\in\Omega
, and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
(i.e.
n>0
) one has

\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)=n\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)=n\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\{s\}\right)\,.

(8.17)

In particular the value does not depend on the particular
\Omega
as long as
s\in\Omega
.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
:
Noting that the empty product is defined (assumed) to equal
1
, e.g. in the case when
\Omega\setminus\{s\}=\emptyset
:

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\right)\\
=&\frac{\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)=n\right)\cdot\prod_{\sigma\in\Omega\setminus\{s\}}\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(\sigma)}(0)\geq 1\right)\cdot\prod_{\sigma\not\in\Omega}\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(\sigma)}(0)=0\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\operatorname{supp}(\vec{\mathbf{N}}(0))=\Omega\right)}\\
=&\frac{\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)=n\right)\cdot\prod_{\sigma\in\Omega\setminus\{s\}}\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(\sigma)}(0)\geq 1\right)\cdot\prod_{\sigma\not\in\Omega}\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(\sigma)}(0)=0\right)}{\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)\geq 1\right)\cdot\prod_{\sigma\in\Omega\setminus\{s\}}\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(\sigma)}(0)\geq 1\right)\cdot\prod_{\sigma\not\in\Omega}\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(\sigma)}(0)=0\right)}\\
=&\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)=n\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(N^{(s)}(0)\geq 1\right)}\,.\end{split}

(8.18)

The last equality can be seen to clearly not depend on the specific
\Omega
, thus the value is the same as long as
s\in\Omega
.
\square

9
Conclusion

Findings and Contributions

I showed how a particular measure of relative fitness can be recast into the statistical framework of average treatment effects.
I explained how (for this problem) violations of positivity assumptions are inevitable in practice, making controlling for confounding difficult.
I gave explicit assumptions under which the corresponding estimands are identifiable from the observed data produced by incubated droplets.

Practical Implications

This chapter demonstrates that this problem has a difficult balance between controlling for confounding and avoiding “data starvation”. This makes it particularly important to be able to predict in advance how much data will be available, using the results from part
II
. By explicitly defining the intended estimands, the specific estimators, and the assumptions required for identifiability from the observed data, this chapter clarifies the goals and limitations of using these estimators.

Next Steps and Open Questions

This chapter points the way forward to formalizing many other statistical estimators that are informally defined in the microbiological literature, even in the context of other experiments. Doing so will hopefully clarify the properties and performance of such estimators. For the analysis of MOREI data specifically, it would be helpful to identify other possible estimands besides those which were described in this chapter. Simulation studies comparing the performance of different estimators would be particularly useful. The next chapter, chapter
8
, addresses an important issue that we need to clarify when comparing the performance of different estimators in a simulation study.

Sparsification

An issue with the estimators proposed in this chapter is their “lack of sparsity”. The values of the estimators from section
6
will almost never be exactly
0
in finite samples, even if they might approach
0
asymptotically. Hence the corresponding estimated interaction network will almost always be a complete graph, even if the magnitudes of many, or even most, of the edges might be small.
One approach to address this “lack of sparsity” issue would be to impose an arbitrary magnitude threshold, below which any estimate values would automatically be set to
0
. That would be problematic for many reasons, not least of which would include deciding on a choice of threshold value and justifying that choice after it has been made. Another approach would be to throw out the average treatment effects framework altogether and use some modification of the sparse regression and variable selection methods mentioned in e.g.
[
LR20
]
to apply to the “gene
\times
gene” interactions problem we have here. Cf. again item
(4)
from section
6.1
. A third approach begins with thinking of sparsity as false discovery control. Then one could attempt to modify the non-parametric method for false discovery control proposed in
[
GCS+21
]
, originally designed for the “gene
\times
environment” interactions problem, to apply to the “gene
\times
gene” interactions problem. This modified method could then be used on the ATE estimators.

Chapter 7. Appendices

\namedsubappendices

Appendix 7.A
Pre-Existing Notions of Positivity Violations in Microbiology Literature

The contrast between the two kinds of positivity violations mentioned in
[
PPG+10
]
appears to be analogous, and perhaps even equivalent, to a similar dichotomy often presented in the existing microbiology literature. When discussing missing data in compositional (i.e. relative abundance) datasets, the authors of
[
PGB21
]
and other sources distinguish between “structural zeros” and “sampling zeros”. It appears that the “structural zeros” (also called “biological zeros”) of
[
PGB21
]
correspond to the situation from
[
PPG+10
]
of when “it may be theoretically impossible for individuals with certain covariate values to receive a given exposure of interest”. Likewise, it appears that the “sampling zeros” (and the related “technical zeros”) of
[
PGB21
]
correspond to the situation from
[
PPG+10
]
of when “violations or near violations of positivity can arise in finite samples due to chance”. This suggests that causal inference is relevant to both of these problems previously documented in the microbiology literature. (Although cf.
[
MGSD19
]
for a different interpretation of “technical zeros”.)

It is also mentioned in
[
PGB21
]
how “methods that consider the conditional dependency structure of microbial networks (like probabilistic graphical models) should perform better… because [they have] the power to resolve direct from indirect associations (e.g. associations between two species mediated through a third species)”. This appears to correspond to the same motivation underlying confounder adjustment in a causal analysis. The main difference here is that
[
PGB21
]
refers to methods using undirected graphical models and outside of any explicit causal framework, whereas causal inference (more typically) relies on methods using directed graphical models (Bayesian networks). Nevertheless, the strength of the analogies here suggests the potential for future work that more directly applies a causal inference framework to make a major, positive impact on the study of microbial interactions.

Appendix 7.B
Combining Estimates from Different Batches

An immediately obvious issue with using the ATE estimands is that they result in different estimates for each batch. Note that, since droplets from different batches were grown for different amounts of time, inherently they are not directly comparable. Especially to the extent that it might be expected that the nature of the interactions could change with time, this could potentially be a useful feature rather than a liability. (See e.g.
[
HFH21
]
for previous work tackling such issues head-on in the case of time series
19
19

19

See
[
CHL+20
]
for an introduction to time series geared towards microbial ecologists.

i.e. longitudinal data.) Collaborators have expressed interest in possibly estimating such functions of time. Readers interested in such approaches should look into the field of functional data analysis, cf. e.g. the monographs
[
RHG09
]
,
[
KR21
]
,
[
RS05
]
,
[
HK12
]
,
[
HE15
]
, or
[
Gre81
]
for an introduction. Either of the two reviews
[
WCM16
]
or
[
UF13
]
may also be helpful, if less thorough.

7.B.1
Criteria for Choosing Aggregation Method

In this preliminary work the goal will be to choose an aggregate summary of these estimates which

1.

is structurally and conceptually simple,

2.

is easy to implement,

3.

sensibly incorporates information from all batches.

This is so that any chosen summary method can be and is likely to be used in practice, even by practitioners without extensive quantitative training.

7.B.2
Why Incorporate Information from All Batches

Given that information from different batches will generally correspond to different microbial growth phases (lag, exponential, stationary), incorporating information from all batches is questionable. After all, arguably from a biological standpoint the growth behavior during exponential phase is what we would most want to quantify. Nevertheless, I still suggest that information from all batches be incorporated, regardless of whether they may be in lag or stationary phases. This is for at least two reasons.

First, on theoretical or “philosophical” grounds, any statistical estimator which is unable to utilize potentially informative data (thus requiring that said data essentially be “thrown out”) just because said data may be in some sense “imperfect” for learning about the problem at hand (e.g. potentially lag or stationary phase batches) is “inefficient”.
