will
satisfy the sparsity-savviness property.

8.B.9
Continuity with respect to Magnitude

Consider the plane (
\mathbb{R}^{2}
) with coordinates denoted
(a,i)
, “
a
” for “actual”, “
i
” for “inferred”. The first coordinate corresponds to the weight of the edge of the actual network in a single-edge comparison, whereas the second coordinate corresponds to the weight of the edge of the inferred network in a single-edge comparison.

Then we can divide/partition the plane into
4
regions:

1.

\{(a,i):(a>0\text{ and }i>0)\text{ or }(a<0\text{ and }i<0)\}\,.

(8.B.22)

These are the non-trivial sign agreements.
Geometrically on the plane, these correspond to the interiors of the upper right and lower left quadrants.

2.

\{(a,i):(a>0\text{ and }i<0)\text{ or }(a<0\text{ and }i>0)\}\,.

(8.B.23)

These are the non-trivial sign disagreements.
Geometrically on the plane these correspond to the interiors of the upper left and lower right quadrants.

3.

\{(a,i):(a=0\text{ and }i\not=0)\text{ or }(a\not=0\text{ and }i=0)\}\,.

(8.B.24)

These are the trivial sign disagreements.
Geometrically on the plane these correspond to the union of the
a
and
i
axes with the origin removed.

4.

\{(a,i):a=0\text{ and }i=0\}=\{(0,0)\}\,.

(8.B.25)

These are the trivial sign agreements.
Geometrically on the plane these correspond to the origin.

Figure
8.B.7
illustrates this heuristically:

Figure 8.B.7
:
The “single-edge comparison plane” described in the main text.

Now consider any continuous function
[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
t\mapsto(a(t),i(t))
, a so-called
path
. For any such path, we can look at the sequence of above regions that it passes through. Close inspection reveals that the set of all such allowable sequences corresponds to the set of all possible paths (in the graph-theoretic sense) in the undirected network from figure
8.B.2
. (At least when prohibiting diagonal movements in the immediate vicinity of the origin, something which is admittedly better motivated in the unweighted case.)

The continuity/composability property, at least intuitively speaking or heuristically or roughly, corresponds to requiring that for any such path in the “single-edge comparison plane”, and any given objective function, its values are continuous as a function of the
t
-parametrized family of graph comparisons corresponding to any given edge in the graph-comparison, and the start and end points.

Specifically, say we are comparing the actual graph
\mathcal{G}_{*}
and the inferred graph
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
. We focus on some particular edge
e\in[S]\times[S]
. This corresponds to a tuple
(a,i)=(e_{*},\hat{e})
, where
e_{*}:=A_{\mathcal{G}_{*}}(e)
and
\hat{e}:=A_{\hat{\mathcal{G}}}(e)
. Then we can choose some path
[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
(e_{*},\hat{e})
as the starting point, i.e.
(a(0),i(0))=(e_{*},\hat{e})
. Then this gives us a parameterized family of tuples of networks, where each
t\in[0,1]
gets sent to
(\mathcal{G}_{*}(t),\hat{\mathcal{G}}(t))
, again with
(\mathcal{G}_{*}(0),\hat{\mathcal{G}}(0))=(\mathcal{G}_{*},\hat{\mathcal{G}})
, and where
A_{\mathcal{G}_{*}(t)}(e)=a(t)
and
A_{\hat{\mathcal{G}}(t)}(e)=i(t)
(the values of the adjacency function are assumed unchanged for all other edges). Composing this parameterized family of tuples of networks with our objective function, we are asking for the composite function
[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
to be continuous, irrespective of which edge we choose, and which path
[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
was chosen.

Call such a path “coordinate-wise non-decreasing” if, for all
t_{1}<t_{2}
in
[0,1]
, we have both
a(t_{1})\leq a(t_{2})

and

i(t_{1})\leq i(t_{2})
.

Of course, when we consider unweighted network, there is no longer any “continuous plane” of pairs of edge weight values to send paths into. But we still would like to consider “discretized paths” where the only allowed “discretized paths” correspond again to paths (in the graph-theoretic sense) in the undirected network from figure
8.B.2
.

Also of course in the case of weighted networks, non-trivial sign agreements are no longer straightforward. In other words, even when the signs (non-trivially) agree, we can now still have differences in magnitude which we would usually want to penalize as well. The only places in the region of non-trivial sign agreements where we don’t have magnitude disagreements are along the intersection with the line
a=i
. This corresponds to a line with slope
1
. In general, any line with slope bounded in between
0
and
+\infty
passes through the two quadrants defining the region of non-trivial sign agreements and thus corresponds to a certain departure (possibly null when the slope is
1
) from absence of magnitude of disagreement. (Hypothetically we could choose to associate this with the non-negative projective line, but that’s probably a topic for another time.) In the limits as the slopes approach either
0
or
+\infty
, we have that the non-trivial sign agreements actually approach trivial sign disagreements. Therefore, continuity combined with the desire to penalize trivial sign disagreements
require us to penalize at least some non-trivial sign agreements
. (Again this is restricted only to the case of weighted networks, which is the only context where non-zero magnitude differences are possible for non-trivial sign agreements.)

While all of this certainly poses some complications for the sparsity-savviness principle, in particular probably requiring that it can only be satisfied in some asymptotic sense which we won’t bother to attempt to precisely define, it actually does not pose any challenges or complications for satisfying a (continuous version of) the double penalization principle. To discuss this, it probably makes sense to begin by discussing a continuous version of monotonicity (i.e. comparing trivial sign agreements with trivial sign disagreements in the case of weighted networks). The main idea however is that (the continuous version of
52
52

52

But then presumably also as a consequence the discretized version
) the double penalization principle does not require any constraints on the behavior of paths passing through the quadrants corresponding to non-trivial sign agreements. It only regulates paths which pass from trivial sign-disagreements to non-trivial sign disagreements, to ensure they display analogous monotonicity behavior to paths from trivial sign agreements to trivial sign disagreements. (I.e. how the objective function behaves in the upper right and lower left quadrants is irrelevant.)

Consider any path starting from
(0,0)
and such that either
a(t)=0
or
i(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,1]
(but not both). Then for the coordinate of the path that is not constant and identically
0
, assume that it is monotone (non-decreasing or non-increasing, it does not matter which). Then any dissimilarity
\mathscr{D}
satisfying the continuous monotonicity property will monotonically
non-decrease
along any such path. I.e. making one of the edge counterparts being compared increasingly nonzero should only worsen the score.

Now the double penalization principle is motivated by the fact that, when looking at any point in the non-trivial sign disagreement quadrants (lower right or upper left), projecting the point onto the
closed
quadrant
\{a\geq 0\text{ and }i\geq 0\}
represents the point as a trivial disagreement for the positive part of the network, whereas projecting the point onto the
closed
quadrant
\{a\leq 0\text{ and }i\leq 0\}
portrays it as a trivial disagreement for the negative part of the network. So intuitively speaking, we would like, at least for paths which are suitably coordinate-wise monotone restricted within one of the non-trivial sign disagreement quadrants, for the objective function to “obey monotonicity for both the positive and negative parts”, i.e. for a dissimilarity function to monotonically non-decrease. Again, this is not at all a restriction on the behavior in the non-trivial sign agreement quadrants and thus whether the objective satisfies the double penalization principle is completely independent of e.g. the relative weighting of sign errors versus magnitude errors. Cf. the figure
8(a)
, where the lighter shaded areas indicate the regions that the (strong and continuous) double penalization principle implies should correspond to greater dissimilarity values than the corresponding large white dots.

(a)

Large white dot has smallest dissimilarity compared to everything in the corresponding lightened regions. Additionally white dots indicate the direction of “increasing isoclines of dissimilarity”.

(b)

Examples of paths to which the continuous double penalization principle applies. Large white dots are the start points of the paths, arrows indicate the end points and directions of the paths.

Figure 8.B.8
:
Schematic depiction of continuous version of double penalization principle.

In the lower right quadrant, a suitable path to which the continuous version of the double penalization principle applies is one for which
a(0)\geq 0
and
i(0)\leq 0
, and then for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1]
with
t_{1}<t_{2}
,
a(t_{1})\leq a(t_{2})
and
i(t_{1})\geq i(t_{2})
. (If the path coordinate functions are differentiable, then basically we are requiring that
a^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
whereas
i^{\prime}(t)\leq 0
.) Likewise, in the upper left quadrant, suitable paths are those for which
a(0)\leq 0
and
i(0)\geq 0
and such that for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1]
with
t_{1}<t_{2}
we have
a(t_{1})\geq a(t_{2})
and
i(t_{1})\leq i(t_{2})
. (If the path coordinate functions are differentiable, then basically we are requiring that
a^{\prime}(t)\leq 0
and
i^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
.) Then for all such paths, the (continuous version of) the double penalization principle requires that a dissimilarity function monotonically non-decrease with
t
. (I feel like requiring the objective to be continuous along with requiring the discretized definition above should be equivalent to this continuous version of the double penalization principle, but if it’s true the formal argument would be very technical and boring so I at least currently will not bother.) Notice in particular that the continuous version of the double penalization principle does
not
require the dissimilarity to increase (non-decrease) fastest or slowest along a specific choice of this class of paths, only that it be monotonically non-decreasing along all of them. (If we assume the start and end points of all of the paths under consideration are the same, then only the “rate of change” along the paths can differ, because obviously then the net difference between the values of the dissimilarity function at the beginning and end of the path must be the same.)

The figure
8(b)
indicates examples of such allowable trajectories.

8.B.10
Considerations for Generalized Networks

The arguments and notions of e.g. the monotonicity principle, double penalization principle, or sparsity-savviness principle, do not inherently need the comparison functions be defined for “discrete”, finite networks. To clarify the “true” underlying ideas, what follows is a preliminary attempt to demonstrate their generalizability to other comparison functions. Hopefully this will be a preliminary step towards future work that makes all of the concepts and principles expressed in this chapter precise enough to be falsifiable.

8.B.10.1
Generalized Edge Spaces

Let a locally compact Polish space
53
53

53

Equivalent to a locally compact second-countable Hausdorff space (a.k.a. “LCCB” space), among other characterizations.

E
correspond to a “generalized edge space”.

Examples:

•

For a (finite) network,
E=[S]\times[S]
(equipped with the usual discrete
54
54

54

Like the discrete topology for
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
, this is also the same as the Euclidean subspace topology inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

topology).

•

For an infinite network,
E=\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
(equipped with the usual discrete topology).

•

For a graphon,
E=[0,1]\times[0,1]
(equipped with the usual Euclidean subspace topology).

8.B.10.2
Generalized Edge/Adjacency Functions

Given a “generalized edge space
E
”, i.e. a locally compact Polish space, a “generalized edge (adjacency) function”
A:E\to\mathbb{R}
will be interpreted as a
continuous
function
E\to\mathbb{R}
.

Note that in the case that the topology of
E
is discrete, then all functions
E\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous. However, this is a meaningful restriction e.g. in the graphon case. Analysis extending these notions to general Borel-measurable functions (or showing that such an extension is impossible) is left to future work.

Note that because
E
is locally compact Polish, and
\mathbb{R}
is Polish, the space of all continuous functions
C(E,\mathbb{R})
is itself a Polish space when equipped with the standard compact-open topology and, moreover, is an exponential object in the category of topological spaces (in particular evaluation of functions is continuous).

8.B.10.3
Indicators as Pointwise Limits

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a topological space,
Y\subseteq X
. Assume that the indicator function
\mathbb{I}\!\left\{Y\right\}:X\to[0,1]
can be written as the monotone non-decreasing pointwise limit of some sequence of continuous functions
f_{n}:X\to[0,1]
, i.e. for all
x\in X
one has
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(x)=\mathbb{I}\!\left\{Y\right\}(x)
and for all
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
,
f_{n_{1}}(x)\leq f_{n_{2}}(x)
. Then
Y
must be (1) open and (2)
F_{\sigma}
.

Proof of Lemma
8.7

First, note that
\{1\}
and
\{0\}
are closed subsets of
[0,1]
, whereas as
[0,1)
and
(0,1]
are open subsets
[0,1]
.

By definition, the inverse image of
\{1\}
under
\mathbb{I}\!\left\{Y\right\}
is exactly the set
Y
, so the sets of values where the
f_{n}
equal
1
must approach
Y
. Because the
f_{n}
are monotone increasing and bounded above by
1
, for
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
we must have
f_{n_{1}}^{-1}(\{1\})\subseteq f_{n_{2}}^{-1}(\{1\})
, i.e. the inverse images of
\{1\}
under the
f_{n}
must be non-decreasing as well and thus “approach
Y
from the inside”. More concretely:

Y=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}^{-1}(\{1\})=\left(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}^{-1}([0,1))\right)^{c}\,,

(8.B.26)

with the last equality following from DeMorgan’s Law. Because all of the
f_{n}
are continuous, the
f_{n}^{-1}(\{1\})
are all closed and the
f_{n}^{-1}([0,1))
are all open. Hence
Y
is the countable union of closed sets, thus
F_{\sigma}
. (Equivalently
Y
is the complement of a countable intersection of open sets, i.e. the complement of a
G_{\delta}
set.)

At the same time, the inverse image of
\{0\}
under
\mathbb{I}\!\left\{Y\right\}
is the set
Y^{c}
, so the sets of values where the
f_{n}
equal
0
must approach
Y^{c}
. Because the
f_{n}
are monotone increasing and bounded below by
0
, for
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
we must have
f_{n_{1}}^{-1}(\{0\})\supseteq f_{n_{2}}^{-1}(\{0\})
, i.e. the inverse images of
\{0\}
under the
f_{n}
must be non-increasing and thus “approach
Y^{c}
from the outside”. More concretely:

Y=(Y^{c})^{c}=\left(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}^{-1}(\{0\})\right)^{c}=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}^{-1}((0,1])\,,

(8.B.27)

with the last equality again following from DeMorgan’s Law. Again because all of the
f_{n}
are continuous, the
f_{n}^{-1}(\{0\})
are all closed and the
f_{n}^{-1}((0,1])
are all open. The arbitrary intersection of closed sets is closed, and the arbitrary union of open sets is open, so (
8.B.27
) means that
Y
is the complement of a closed set, or equivalently an open set.
\square

Corollary 3
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a topological space,
Z\subseteq X
. Assume that the indicator function
\mathbb{I}\!\left\{Z\right\}:X\to[0,1]
can be written as the monotone non-increasing pointwise limit of some sequence of continuous functions
g_{n}:X\to[0,1]
, i.e. for all
x\in X
one has
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}g_{n}(x)=\mathbb{I}\!\left\{Z\right\}(x)
and for all
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
,
g_{n_{1}}(x)\geq g_{n_{2}}(x)
. Then
Z
must be (1) closed and (2)
G_{\delta}
.

Proof of Corollary
3
:
Take
Y:=Z^{c}
and
f_{n}:=1-g_{n}
. These satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
8.7
. Because
Y=Z^{c}
is open and
F_{\sigma}
, it follows that its complement
Z
must be closed and
G_{\delta}
.
\square

8.B.10.4
\sigma
-compact subsets

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
X
be a
\sigma
-compact and Hausdorff
55
55

55

Hausdorffness is technically somewhat overkill. Because a closed subset of a compact set is always compact, all we need is for all compact sets to be closed (a so-called “KC space”), and even then only for one direction. That being said, Hausdorffness is a much weaker assumption than the assumption that
E
be a locally compact Polish space.

space. Then a subset
Y
is
\sigma
-compact
56
56

56

Because compactness is an “intrinsic” topological property, i.e.
Z\subseteq X
is compact in its subspace topology if and only if it is compact in the “ambient” topology of
X
, the same is also true of
\sigma
-compactness. For other properties we would need to be more careful and specify whether we meant in the subspace topology of
Y
or in the “ambient” topology of
X
.

if and only if
Y
is
F_{\sigma}
.

Proof of Lemma
8.8

(\implies)
Assume
Y
is
\sigma
-compact, i.e.
Y=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\tilde{K}_{n}
with all
\tilde{K}_{n}
compact. Because the
\tilde{K}_{n}
are compact, they are closed. So
Y
is the countable union of closed sets, i.e.
F_{\sigma}
.

(\impliedby)
Assume
Y
is
F_{\sigma}
, i.e.
Y=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}F_{n}
with all
F_{n}
closed. Because
X
is
\sigma
-compact,
X=\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}K_{m}
with all
K_{m}
compact. Then because
X\cap Y=Y
, we have that

Y=\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}(K_{m}\cap F_{n})\,.

Because the
K_{m}
are compact and the
F_{n}
are closed, for all
m
and
n
we have that
K_{m}\cap F_{n}
is compact. Therefore
Y
is the countable union of compact sets, i.e.
\sigma
-compact.
\square

8.B.10.5
Relevant Facts

•

Locally compact Polish spaces are (by definition) metrizable, and metrizable spaces are perfectly normal (because they are normal and closed sets are
G_{\delta}
).

•

Locally compact and second-countable implies
\sigma
-compact, all Polish spaces are second-countable, so locally compact Polish spaces are
\sigma
-compact.

•

In a metrizable space open sets are
57
57

57

This is dual to the aforementioned fact that closed sets are
G_{\delta}
.

F_{\sigma}
, thus by Lemma
8.8
and the above facts we know that every open subset of a locally compact Polish space is
\sigma
-compact.

8.B.10.6
Approximating Open Indicators from Below

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
U
be an open subset of a locally compact Polish space
E
. Then there exists a monotone non-decreasing sequence of continuous functions
f_{n}:E\to[0,1]
such that the indicator
\mathbb{I}\!\left\{U\right\}:E\to[0,1]
is the pointwise limit, i.e. for all
e\in E
one has
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(e)=\mathbb{I}\!\left\{U\right\}(e)
and for all
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
,
f_{n_{1}}(e)\leq f_{n_{2}}(e)
. (Using Lemma
8.7
it follows that the existence of such a sequence exactly characterizes open subsets of
E
.)

Proof of Lemma
8.9

This seems to only
58
58

58

Specifically that
E
be perfectly normal. However,
E
is perfectly normal if and only if it is normal and every closed set is
G_{\delta}
, every closed set is
G_{\delta}
if and only if every open set is
F_{\sigma}
, so
E
is perfectly normal if and only if it is normal and all open subsets are
F_{\sigma}
. In particular metrizability is not directly relevant here, even though all metrizable spaces are perfectly normal. That being said, known counterexamples of perfectly normal spaces that are not metrizable, e.g. the Arens-Fort space or the Sorgenfrey line, are all fairly “weird” and thus not likely to be relevant to the practice of data science.

require that
E
be normal and that all open subsets be
F_{\sigma}
. First, using the fact that
U
open is
F_{\sigma}
, write
U=\bigcup\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}F_{n}
with all
F_{n}
closed
59
59

59

We can assume without loss of generality that the sequence of closed sets is increasing, by taking
\tilde{F}_{n}:=\bigcup\limits_{m\leq n}F_{m}
if necessary, noting the fact that the union of finitely many closed sets is again always closed.
. Next follows an induction argument.

For the base case where
n=1
, note that the fact that
E
is perfectly normal means Urysohn’s lemma can be applied to the closed sets
F_{1}
and
U^{c}
in such a way to give a continuous function
f_{1}:=g_{1}:E\to[0,1]
such that
g_{1}(e)=0
for all
e\in U^{c}
,
g_{1}(e)=1
for all
e\in F_{1}
, and (this is the part requiring
E
to be perfectly normal)
0<g_{1}(e)<1
for all
e\in U\setminus F_{1}
. Because there are no
n
smaller than
1
, the monotonicity requirement is vacuously satisfied.

Now assume for
n\geq 1
the induction hypothesis holds that for all
m\leq n
there exists a continuous
f_{m}:E\to[0,1]
such that
f_{m}(e)=0
for all
e\in U^{c}
,
f_{m}(e)=1
for all
e\in F_{m}
,
0<f_{m}(e)<1
for all
e\in U\setminus F_{m}
, and that for all
n_{1},n_{2}\leq n
with
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
that one has for all
e\in E
that
f_{n_{1}}(e)\leq f_{n_{2}}(e)
. To prove the claim, we now need to show that there exists a continuous
f_{n+1}:E\to[0,1]
such that
f_{m+1}=0
for all
e\in U^{c}
,
f_{n+1}(e)=1
for all
e\in F_{n+1}
,
0<f_{n+1}(e)<1
for all
e\in U\setminus F_{n+1}
, and that for all
m\leq n
that for all
e\in E
one has
f_{m}(e)\leq f_{n+1}(e)
.

Because
E
is perfectly normal, again Urysohn’s lemma can be applied to the closed sets
F_{n+1}
and
U^{c}
in such a way to give a continuous function
g_{n+1}:E\to[0,1]
such that
g_{n+1}(e)=0
for all
e\in U^{c}
,
g_{n+1}(e)=1
for all
e\in F_{n+1}
, and
0<g_{n+1}(e)<1
for all
e\in U\setminus F_{n+1}
. Now define
f_{n+1}:=\max\{g_{n+1},f_{n}\}
. As the maximum of two continuous functions,
f_{n+1}
is itself continuous. Likewise, because for all
e\in U^{c}
we have that
f_{n}(e)=g_{n+1}(e)=0
, we also have that
f_{n+1}(e)=0
for all
e\in U^{c}
. Because
f_{n}(e)\leq 1
for all
e\in E
, and
g_{n+1}(e)=1
for all
e\in F_{n+1}
, it follows that for all
e\in F_{n+1}
one has

f_{n+1}(e)=\max\{f_{n}(e),g_{n+1}(e)\}=\max\{f_{n}(e),1\}=1\,.

Finally, because
F_{n}\subseteq F_{n+1}
, we have that
U\setminus F_{n+1}\subseteq U\setminus F_{n}
, so
0<f_{n}(e)<1
for all
e\in U\setminus F_{n}
in turn implies that
0<f_{n}(e)<1
for all
e\in U\setminus F_{n+1}
. Therefore

0<f_{n+1}(e)=\max\{f_{n}(e),g_{n+1}(e)\}<1

for all
e\in U\setminus F_{n+1}
. Therefore we have proven the induction step.

The only claim that remains to be verified is that for all
e\in E
one has
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(e)=\mathbb{I}\!\left\{U\right\}(e)
. This of course splits into two cases: first verifying that, for all
e\in U^{c}
, that
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(e)=\mathbb{I}\!\left\{U\right\}(e)=0
, and second verifying that, for all
e\in U
, that
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(e)=\mathbb{I}\!\left\{U\right\}(e)=1
.

For the first verification, note that by definition of the
f_{n}
we have that for all
e\in U^{c}
one has
f_{n}(e)=0
. Therefore for every
e\in U^{c}
the corresponding sequence
\{f_{n}(e)\}
is the constant sequence
0
, which obviously converges and with
0
as its limit.

For the second verification observe that, because the
F_{n}
are assumed to be increasing, i.e.
F_{n+1}\supseteq F_{n}
for all
n
,
and
that
\bigcup\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}F_{n}=U
(i.e. we can’t have the
F_{n}
“stabilize” at some proper subset of
U
) that for every
e\in U
there must exist some
n_{e}
such that for all
n\geq n_{e}
,
e\in F_{n}
. Thus for all
n\geq n_{e}
, by definition of the
f_{n}
we have that
f_{n}(e)=1
. Thus for every
e\in U
the sequence
\{f_{n}(e)\}
equals
1
for all but finitely many terms, again obviously forcing the sequence to converge and to the limit
1
.

Comments about Approximating Open Indicators from Below

In the case that
E
is locally compact Polish, as explained above it follows that
E
is both locally compact and
\sigma
-compact. Because the finite union of compact sets is again compact, being
\sigma
-compact is equivalent to equaling an
increasing
countable union of compact sets. Because any open subset of a locally compact space is again locally compact, any open subset of
E
is locally compact. Likewise, in a locally compact Polish space any open set is
F_{\sigma}
and
F_{\sigma}
subsets of all
\sigma
-compact spaces are themselves
\sigma
-compact, thus any open subset of
E
is also
\sigma
-compact. Therefore we can assume without loss of generality in the case that
E
is a locally compact Polish space that the increasing sequence of closed sets whose union equals the open subset
U
consists exclusively of compact sets, and that for each
n
we have
K_{n+1}\supseteq Int(K_{n+1})\supseteq K_{n}
, where
Int(K_{n+1})
denotes the topological interior (with respect to either
E
or
U
, both notions coincide because
U
is open). Such “(strong) exhaustions by compact sets” can be technically useful.

8.B.10.7
Use for Censoring Subsets

Let
U
be an open subset of a “generalized edge space”
E
, i.e. a locally compact Polish space
E
. Given a monotonically non-decreasing sequence of continuous functions
f_{n}:E\to[0,1]
which converge pointwise to the indicator function of
U
, it follows that the sequence of functions
1-f_{n}
are a non-increasing sequence of continuous functions which converge pointwise to the indicator function of
U^{c}
. If we replace/augment such a convergent sequence to get a convergent net indexed by the directed set
[0,1]
, then we get non-increasing (as
t
in creases) functions that converge pointwise to the indicator of
U^{c}
as
t\to 1
. Re-indexing by
1-t
, we get non-decreasing (as
t
increases) functions that converge pointwise to the indicator of
U^{c}
as
t\to 0
. The details of this will be explained below, but this is to be interpreted as progressively removing the “censoring” of the subset
U
as
t
increases, approaching no censoring as
t\to 1
, and full censoring as
t\to 0
. While the important aspect for definitions is that the “easing of censoring” is done monotonically, it is also nice to know that this can be constructed in a way that is continuous.

Specifically, first consider the sequence
t_{n}
defined on
[0,1]
, and converging towards
0
, defined for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
60
60

60

This is probably the simplest example, but any monotonically decreasing sequence bounded within
[0,1]
with
t_{0}=1
and whose limit is
0
will work.

t_{n}=2^{-n}
. Define “censoring functions”
\xi_{t}:E\to[0,1]
for all
t\in[0,1]
as follows. First,
\xi_{0}
is the constant function identically equal to
1
for all
e\in E
. Then for all
n\geq 1
, define
\xi_{t_{n}}
to be
\xi_{t_{n}}:=1-f_{n}
. Then for any other value of
t\in[0,1]
, first identify the
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
t_{m}=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\{t_{n}:t_{n}<t\}
. Such an
m
must exist because the
t_{n}
are monotonically decreasing and thus there can only be finitely many of them greater than
t
. Then basically define
\xi_{t}
as the corresponding “interpolation” between
\xi_{t_{m-1}}
and
\xi_{t_{m}}
, i.e.

\xi_{t}=\frac{(t_{m}-t)}{(t_{m}-t_{m-1})}\xi_{t_{m-1}}+\frac{(t_{m}-t_{m-1})-(t_{m}-t)}{(t_{m}-t_{m-1})}\xi_{t_{m}}\,.

From these definitions it follows that:

•

the
\xi_{t}
are all continuous for
t>0
(being the convex combination of continuous functions),

•

as
t\to 1
the
\xi_{t}
approach the constant function identically equal to
1
,

•

as
t\to 0
the
\xi_{t}
approach the (discontinuous) indicator function of
U^{c}
(which equals
0
on
U
and
1
everywhere else),

•

the
\xi_{t}
are monotonically non-decreasing as
t
increases, i.e. for all
t_{1}<t_{2}
we have for all
e\in E
that
\xi_{t_{1}}(e)\leq\xi_{t_{2}}(e)
.

Thus the
\xi_{t}
define a continuous “homotopy” from complete censoring of
U
as
t\to 0
to no “censoring” at all as
t\to 1
. For
t>0
, the
\xi_{t}\in C(E,\mathbb{R})
(i.e. are continuous), so their graph-loci are closed subsets of
E\times\mathbb{R}
. Thus I expect, i.e. conjecture and have not rigorously verified, that we would have a homotopy, in the rigorous sense of a continuous function
[0,1]\to(E\times\mathbb{R}
) connecting the graph-loci of the
\xi_{t}
for
t>0
with (as
t\to 0
) the closed subset of
E\times\mathbb{R}
that equals
(U^{c}\times\{1\})\cup(\partial(U)\times[0,1])\cup(U\times\{0\})
(which contains, but is a strict superset of, the graph-locus of the discontinuous indicator function of
U
). Here
\partial(U)
equals the topological boundary of
U
, i.e. the closure of
U
minus the interior of
U
, or equivalently
U^{c}
(which is closed and thus equals its own closure) minus its interior (because the topological boundary of any subset equals that of its complement).

8.B.10.8
Monotone Uncensoring of
U\subseteq E

Definition 8.B.10.1
.

A family of functions
(\xi_{t}:E\to\mathbb{R})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that

•

for all
t>0
we have
\xi_{t}\in C(E,\mathbb{R})
(i.e.
\xi_{t}
is continuous),

•

the graph-loci of the
\xi_{t}
“vary continously with
t
,

•

as
t\to 1
the
\xi_{t}
approach the constant function
1:E\to\mathbb{R}
pointwise,

•

as
t\to 0
the
\xi_{t}
approach the indicator function of
U^{c}
pointwise,

•

the
\xi_{t}
are pointwise monotonically non-decreasing as
t
increases.

I claim to have demonstrated previously that such a monotone uncensoring exists for any open subset
U\subseteq E
, and that satisfying the first condition (that the
\xi_{t}
be continuous for
t>0
) requires that
U\subseteq E
be open.

8.B.10.9
Comments about Monotone Uncensorings

Future work should probably seek to determine whether there is a sensible way to require the
\xi_{t}
for
t>0
to only be Borel-measurable, rather than necessarily continuous. Again, we showed above that “continuous” monotone uncensorings should only exist for open subsets of
E
, so one question such future work would have to address is which kinds of subsets of
E
would admit “measurable” monotone uncensorings.

The definition of (continuous) monotone uncensoring is used to define the (continuous versions of the) monotonicity property and double penalization property for comparison functions of (generalized) networks. Thus it is important to verify that such families of functions actually do exist.

When
E
has the discrete topology (and thus every subset is open), the condition that
U
be open is obviously not at all restrictive.

Appendix A.1
Relevant Background on Differential Equations

The goals of the simulations were to follow the precedent of prior work
[
VCF+18
]

[
FM14
]

[
MBH+14
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
MMV+08
]
in postulating a likelihood model similar to the generalized Lotka-Volterra equations (cf. again section
7.5
), while also being more biologically realistic in both (i) not having any restrictions on interaction coefficients (which would seem to be arbitrary from a biological point of view), and (ii) addressing the capacity for nutrient depletion and waste product accumulation to moderate changes in population size, something which seems especially relevant, and even inappropriate to ignore, inside of the highly resource-limited (oligotrophic) environment of a microfluidic droplet.

1
Exponential Growth: Unlimited Resources

The exponential differential equation describes the idealized situation where the rate of change in the population is directly proportional to the current number of cells:

\frac{\mathrm{d}n}{\mathrm{d}t}=rn\,,

(1.1)

which is equivalent (for populations with more than zero cells) to the even simpler equation:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n)}{\mathrm{d}t}=r\,.

(1.2)

The second form emphasizes how this idealized situation corresponds to what is often referred to as a constant growth (or decay) rate.

2
Nutrient Limitation and Indirect Interactions

The exponential model is often considered adequate for describing the growth under copiotrophic conditions immediately following the initial lag phase. However, since the effects of nutrient depletion and waste product accumulation increase with the size of the population, for large enough populations it becomes unrealistic to model the population size’s effect on the rate of change as being constant. Instead an additional multiplicative factor can be included to describe how growth is expected to slow down as nutrients are depleted and waste products accumulate:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log x)}{\mathrm{d}t}=r\left(1-\frac{x}{K}\right)\,.

(2.1)

This is called the logistic differential equation. As the population size continues to approach the constant
K
, growth slows down (asymptotically) towards nothing, causing
K
to represent an upper bound on the total possible population size. This upper bound is often referred to as the
carrying capacity
of the environment which supports the population.

Assuming that the population sizes of any given strain do not affect the population sizes of the other strains, it is straightforward to model the simultaneous exponential growth of multiple strains via a system of differential equations:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=r_{s}\,.

(2.2)

When still assuming that the population sizes of different strains do not affect one another, it is also straightforward in exactly the same manner to model the simultaneous logistic growth of multiple strains using a system of differential equations:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=r_{s}\left(1-\frac{n^{(s)}}{K_{s}}\right)\,.

(2.3)

The above system of equations (
2.3
) is unrealistic, however, inasmuch as it assumes not only that the population sizes of different strains do not directly affect one another, but also because it assumes that the environment has a separate carrying capacity for each strain, independent of that for all other strains.

Especially in an environment like the interior of a single droplet in a microfluidics experiment, the same nutrient supply will often be common/shared among all strains, and the accumulation of waste products will affect all strains simultaneously. In other words, in a limited resource environment strains will almost always interact with each other at least
indirectly
, with the
indirect
interactions being mediated by the nutrient supply and concentration of waste products. Therefore it is often more realistic to assume a single carrying capacity for the total aggregate population size of all strains in the environment. The following system of equations better models the aforementioned situation:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=r_{s}\left(1-\frac{\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}}{K}\right)\,,

(2.4)

since as the total aggregate population size of all strains (asymptotically) approaches the common and shared carrying capacity
K
of the environment, the growth of all strains will simultaneously slow (asymptotically) towards zero. It is worth noting though that in practice different strains can have wildly varying metabolisms, such that what constitutes a nutrient or a harmful waste product could differ substantially between strains, and making the above description overly simplistic. For examples of models accounting for the potentially different metabolisms of different strains, see e.g.
[
EVL+22
]
,
[
GWDM21
]
,
[
ESGVS21
]
,
[
ELW+20
]
,
[
GLB+18
]
, or
[
MXS17
]
. (For an overview, see
[
GCLF18
]
.) However, fully addressing this issue falls outside of the scope of the current manuscript and is left for future work.

2.1
Comparison with Previous Work

The paper
[
MBH+14
]
uses equations analogous to (
2.3
), with
K_{s}=K
for all
s
for a common
K
, and claims that this alone is sufficient alone to enforce the constraint that
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\leq K
always. However, that does not seem to be true. As far as I can tell, with an equation of that form alone, there is no reason at all why the (logarithmic) derivatives of all strains should approach
0
as
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\to K
. If the growth rates do not asymptotically approach
0
as the total population sizes
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}
of all strains approaches the supposedly common carrying capacity
K
, then there is no reason to expect in general that
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\leq K
.

For example, based on (
2.3
), as
n^{(s_{1})}\to K/2
and
n^{(s_{2})}\to K/2
the growth rates of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
would not seem to be approach
0
. Yet under those circumstances
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\leq K
. Hence, absent a flaw in the above reasoning, it seems clear that (
2.3
) is incapable of enforcing a common carrying capacity constraint shared by all strains simultaneously.

In contrast, it is obviously the case for the proposed equation (
2.4
) that the growth rates of all strains will asymptotically approach
0
as
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\to K
.

3
Direct Interactions

Even focusing on the initial regime where diminishing nutrient supplies and increasing waste product concentrations have not yet become significant factors affecting growth, the assumption in (
2.2
) that the growth of any given strain’s population is unaffected by the population sizes of other strains can still be unrealistic. This is of course because of
direct
interactions between different strains of microbes, which can either support or inhibit their growth, and which are the primary focus of interest of the current manuscript. The generalized Lotka-Volterra equations seek to account for direct interactions by adding to (
2.2
) a linear interaction term:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=r_{s}+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}n^{(\sigma)}\,.

(3.1)

The model describes interactions between different strains through the magnitude and sign of the coefficients in the portion of the affine expressions above which are a linear combination of the population sizes of all of the strains. Positive coefficients correspond to mutualistic interactions between strains whereas negative coefficients correspond to antagonistic interactions between strains.

4
Combining Indirect and Direct Interactions

The simulations used in this paper seek to capture both indirect and direct interactions between microbial strains by combining models (
2.4
) and (
3.1
) above in a straightforward way:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=\left(r_{s}+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}n^{(\sigma)}\right)\left(1-\frac{\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{(\sigma)}}{K}\right)\,.

(4.1)

This preserves the property that the change in population size will slow towards zero as the total aggregate population size approaches the environment’s carrying capacity, while still retaining ability for the population sizes of various strains to directly affect one another. This makes sense even in the case of antagonistic interactions, since as the growth of the antagonizing strain slows towards zero (along with the growth of all growing strains), one would expect that the magnitude of the antagonizing interaction would also slow towards zero, making it sensible for the decay in the population size in the antagonized strain to slow towards zero as well.

Also, unlike the regular generalized Lotka-Volterra equations, it is intended that, for any possible choice of direct interaction coefficients, the population sizes of any solutions will remain bounded for any initial condition for which the carrying capacity of the aggregate population is not exceeded. (Cf. for example
[
VCF+18
]
which excludes certain combinations of coefficient values to prevent the possibility of blow-up. To me that seems arbitrary from a purely biological point of view.) This is an additional source of realism for (
4.1
), since there does not seem to be any a priori biological reason for why certain combinations of values of interaction coefficients should be prohibited. This quote from
[
MBH+14
]
is precedent for the above viewpoint on parameter constraints:

One approach to improving parameter estimation is to superimpose constraints on the parameters; however, unless these constraints can be justified both biologically and mathematically, they should not be enforced because the results can be greatly affected.

4.1
Comparison with Previous Work

Note that the derivation of (
4.1
) given above is not the first attempt to combine logistic growth constraints with gLV-like direct interactions. Notably
[
MBH+14
]
proposes an equation of the form (modulo logarithms and differences in notation):

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=r_{s}\left(1-\frac{n^{(\sigma)}}{K}\right)+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}n^{(\sigma)}\,.

(4.2)

In section
2.1
above I already explained why the proposed implementation of a common carrying capacity in (
4.2
) does not seem to enforce the intended constraint
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\leq K
. Let us apply the fix proposed in
2.1
, namely switching the attempted carrying capacity implementation from a form similar to (
2.3
) to a form similar instead to (
2.4
):

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=r_{s}\left(1-\frac{\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{(\sigma)}}{K}\right)+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}n^{(\sigma)}\,.

(4.3)

However (
4.3
) also seems to fail to enforce the intended constraint
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\leq K
, for the same reason that (
2.3
) does. Namely, without distributing the “logistic factor” across the entire expression, which
is
done in (
4.1
), again there does not seem to be any reason why we should have for (
4.3
) that all growth rates simultaneously approach
0
as
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\to K
. In contrast, (
4.1
) was explicitly designed so that all growth rates simultaneously approach
0
as
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\to K
. Hence it seems much more plausible to me that (
4.1
) enforces the intended constraint
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{({\sigma})}\leq K
, whereas I do not see why either (
4.2
) or (
4.3
) would. Thus (
4.1
) seems to be an improvement on previous work.

5
Common Framework

All of the above systems of equations fit into the following framework:

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=G_{s}(n^{(1)},\dots,n^{(S)})\left(1-\frac{H_{s}(n^{(1)},\dots,n^{(S)})}{K_{s}}\right)\,,

(5.1)

where
G_{s}
corresponds to the growth of the given strain under idealized conditions, and
H_{s}
is a quantity for which the external environment imposes some finite carrying capacity
K_{s}
, such that
H_{s}\rightarrow K_{s}
causes all changes in the population size of strain
s
to slow towards zero. For equations (
2.2
), (
2.3
), and (
2.4
), one has that
G_{s}=r_{s}
, whereas for equations (
3.1
) and (
4.1
) one has instead that
G_{s}=r_{s}+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}n^{(\sigma)}
. For equations (
2.2
) and (
3.1
) one has that
H_{s}=0
, for equation (
2.3
) one has that
H_{s}=n^{(s)}
, and for equations (
2.4
) and (
4.1
) one has instead that
H_{s}=\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{(\sigma)}
as well as that
K_{s}=K
for all
s\in[S]
for a given fixed constant
K
.

Of course in order for its effect on changes in the population size of strain
s
to have any clear interpretation as an external limitation on the magnitude of changes in the system, one needs that
0\leq H_{s}\leq K_{s}
so that the factor multiplying
G_{s}
is always a value between
0
and
1
. As mentioned already before,
H_{s}=\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{(\sigma)}
satisfies this property in equations (
2.4
) and (
4.1
), or at least for the region of phase space between the two hyperplanes
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{(\sigma)}=0
and
\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{(\sigma)}=K
(recall that
K_{1}=\dots=K_{S}=K
for these equations). This is fine since the aformentioned region is the only biologically realistic/sensible/meaningful region of phase space anyway, and any solution of (
4.1
) beginning in that region will remain inside that region for all time.

6
“Competitive Lotka-Volterra Equations”

There is at least one other similar system of differential equations intended to model both the direct interactions corresponding to the system (
3.1
) and external limitations due to finiteness of nutrient supply and the accumulation of waste products, which is often called the “Competitive Lotka-Volterra equations”. For the competitive Lotka-Volterra equations, using the general framework of (
5.1
) one has

G_{s}=r_{s}\,,\quad\quad\quad H_{s}=\sum_{{\sigma}=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}n^{(\sigma)}\,.

(6.1)

Apart from the issue that
K_{s}
may have different values for different strains (which can easily be rectified), a bigger issue with these equations, and why they were not used for these simulations, is that there is no way to guarantee that
0\leq H_{s}\leq 1
, at least not without restrictions on the interaction coefficients which from a biological point of view would be arbitrary. Moreover
H_{s}=\sum_{{\sigma}=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}n^{(\sigma)}
does not correspond to any scientifically meaningful or experimentally observable quantity, making the meaning of the restrictions imposed on the growth of strain
s
by the
(1-H_{s}/K_{s})
factor difficult, if not impossible, to give any scientifically meaningful interpretation. In contrast,
H_{s}=\sum_{{\sigma}}n^{(\sigma)}
is not only possible to measure experimentally, but is probably easier to measure than any of the
n^{(s)}
individually (since the accurate assignment of microbes’ taxonomic identifications can be challenging to do in a high-throughput manner).

This last reason for not using the competitive Lotka-Volterra equations is more subjective, but it seems that the purpose of the linear term in the generalized Lotka-Volterra equations (
3.1
) is to generalize the constant expression used for
G_{s}
in (
2.2
) with an affine expression instead, while still leaving the absence of constraints on growth unchanged when compared to (
2.2
). Therefore, the competitive Lotka-Volterra equations (
6.1
), in using the linear expression for the growth-constraining term
H_{s}
, and leaving the growth describing factor
G_{s}
as a constant and thus ungeneralized, seems to not follow the “spirit” of the generalized Lotka-Volterra equations (
3.1
), and thus did not seem adequately comparable to prior work
[
VCF+18
]

[
FM14
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
MMV+08
]
using those equations.

Appendix A.2
Simulating Growth of Microbes in Droplets

In practice, observed measurements of the numbers of reads
\vec{\mathbf{R}}_{d}(t_{b})
for each strain in a droplet
d
from batch
b
, meant to correspond to the numbers of cells from each strain currently alive
\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(t_{b})
in droplet
d
at time
t_{b}
, will instead better correspond to the numbers of cells which had been alive at any time before time
t_{b}
(and which have not yet had their genetic material scavenged by other microbes). Compare with Figure
A.2.9
. This discrepancy is because all cells, living or dead, must be lysed in order to be able to sequence the contents of the (previously) living cells. This leads to the contents of any cells which had died before being lysed to most likely also have their genetic material sequenced. One of the goals of the simulations was to explicitly account for this discrepancy between theory and measurements.

Figure A.2.9
:
At the end of the growth period, two cells are still alive in the droplet, but one is dead. After lysing the cells and sequencing their contents, the reported number of reads is three. The dead cell is effectively counted as if it were alive. Cf. figure
5
.

Another goal of these simulations was to explicitly account for the reality of resource limitations in these small droplets, which clearly will not allow for indefinite exponential growth. We required that any solution achieving that goal also not impose any restrictions on the allowed interaction coefficients, since there would not seem to be any biological justification for any such restrictions. In particular, such restrictions seem to most often be made for no other reason than preventing unbounded growth. Thus, in principle, a solution correctly modelling resource limitations should work for any interaction coefficients. Resource limitations inherently limit growth and should be sufficient to do so without artificial constraints on the interaction coefficients. (Cf. the argument in section
4
.)

A further goal was to have a simulation model that was comparable to prior work
[
VCF+18
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
MMV+08
]

[
FM14
]

[
MBH+14
]
, while also not sacrificing accuracy or relevance for this problem in terms of failing to accomplish the first and second goals. For example, although it was found in
[
VCF+18
]
that the generalized Lotka-Volterra equations provided a good approximation to the observed dynamics, this was for manually grown plate cultures which were continuously replated as necessary in order to ensure that the measured populations never encountered resource deprivations. Chapter
A.1
describes in detail how the third and second goals were reconciled.

One benefit of satisfying the last goal above is that it allows looking at the performance of the parametric estimator described in appendix
A.3
in a context which is both “fair” and “realistic”. “Fair” in the sense of being reasonably similar to the models used implicitly in prior work
[
VCF+18
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
MBH+14
]

[
MMV+08
]

[
FM14
]
from which it was derived. ‘‘Realistic’’ in the sense that the original models are still significantly misspecified
61
61

61

I assume that parametric models are almost always misspecified in practice. Cf.
[
vdLR11
]
for discussion.

.

1
Modelling Cell Counts under Nutrient Limitation

Starting from equation (
4.1
), a discretization is applied to define an Euler step update, and then a stochastic noise term
\varepsilon
is added to yield

\begin{split}&\log\left(N^{(s)}(t\!+\!\Delta t)\right)-\log\left(N^{(s)}(t)\right)\\
=&\begin{cases}\left(\Delta t\left(\displaystyle r_{s}+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}N^{(\sigma)}(t)\right)+\varepsilon\right)\frac{\left(\displaystyle K-\sum_{{\sigma}=1}^{S}N^{(\sigma)}(t)\right)}{\displaystyle K}&\text{when }N^{(s)}(t)\not=0\\
0&\text{when }N^{(s)}(t)=0\end{cases}\end{split}

(1.1)

where all of the
\varepsilon
are i.i.d.
\operatorname{Gaussian}(0,\eta^{2}\Delta t)
for some “noise scale”
\eta>0
(the second parameter is the variance, so e.g. the standard deviation is
\eta\sqrt{\Delta t}
).

The equation (
1.1
) has a relationship with (
4.1
) that is analogous to the relationship that the assumed updates (
7.15
) from previous work
[
VCF+18
]

[
MMV+08
]

[
FM14
]

[
SBT+13
]
have with the generalized Lotka-Volterra equations (
3.1
).

This may be equivalent (cf.
[
KP10
, section 6.2 or part IV]
to check my work, because I doubt this is entirely correct) to a discretization of the following SDE model:

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}N^{(s)}(t)\\
=&\begin{cases}N^{(s)}(t)\left(\mathrm{d}t\left(\displaystyle r_{s}+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}N^{(\sigma)}(t)\right)+\eta\mathrm{d}W(t)\right)\frac{\left(\displaystyle K-\sum_{{\sigma}=1}^{S}N^{(\sigma)}(t)\right)}{\displaystyle K}&\text{when }N^{(s)}(t)\not=0\\
0&\text{when }N^{(s)}(t)=0\end{cases}\end{split}

(1.2)

where
\mathrm{d}W(t)
denotes a standard Wiener process/Brownian motion.

Using the notation of equation (
5.1
) from chapter
A.1
, the update (
1.1
) can be written as

\log\left(N^{(s)}(t\!+\!\Delta t)\right)-\log\left(N^{(s)}(t)\right)=\begin{cases}\left((\Delta t)G_{s}+\varepsilon\right)\frac{K-H_{s}}{K}&\text{when }N^{(s)}(t)\not=0\\
0&\text{when }N^{(s)}(t)=0\end{cases}

(1.3)

using the definitions

\begin{split}G_{s}=&r_{s}+\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}N^{(\sigma)}(t)\\
H_{s}=&\sum_{{\sigma}=1}^{S}N^{(\sigma)}(t)\end{split}\,.

(1.4)

It is possible, due to the errors introduced by both discretization and stochasticization, that the population of a droplet in the simulation might temporarily exceed the carrying capacity, which corresponds to a special case of
\operatorname{sign}(K\!-\!H_{s})=-1
. Without modification to equation (
1.1
), this would lead to a biologically extremely unrealistic indefinite explosion in population growth for any strain
s
whose growth due to microbial interactions
G_{s}
was negative (i.e. decreasing growth). Defining

\varphi(x,y):=\begin{cases}-\operatorname{sign}(x)\operatorname{sign}(y)&\operatorname{sign}(x)=\operatorname{sign}(y)=-1\\
1&\text{else}\end{cases}\,,

(1.5)

the aforementioned problem is fixed by using the update equation

\log\left(N^{(s)}(t\!+\!\Delta t)\right)-\log\left(N^{(s)}(t)\right)=\begin{cases}\varphi(G_{s},(K\!-\!H_{s}))\left((\Delta t)G_{s}+\varepsilon\right)\frac{K\!-\!H_{s}}{K}&N^{(s)}(t)\not=0\\
0&N^{(s)}(t)=0\end{cases}

(1.6)

whose corresponding system of differential equations

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\log n^{(s)})}{\mathrm{d}t}=\varphi(G_{s},(K-H_{s}))G_{s}(n^{(1)},\dots,n^{(S)})\left(1-\frac{H_{s}(n^{(1)},\dots,n^{(S)})}{K}\right)\,,

(1.7)

has solutions which vary smoothly in phase space except for a region of measure zero, where they still vary continuously. Thus any concerns about the realism of equation (
1.3
) due to non-smoothness seem likely to be minor in comparison to e.g. the known potential issues for correctly modelling the dynamics of even the unmodified generalized Lotka-Volterra equations (
3.1
) in low dimensions
[
LE01
]
(i.e. few strains, e.g. 2-3) introduced by discretization (especially via Euler steps, cf. e.g.
[
Ush82
]
) or stochasticization (discussed e.g. in the case of continuous time SDE models in
[
Ara03
]
). Empirically, it seems that with
\Delta t
chosen to be small enough, the dynamics should (almost) always be constrained to those of (
1.3
), so the dynamics in (
1.6
) can be considered a fallback or last-resort failsafe that usually is irrelevant but nevertheless guarantees no unbounded growth.

2
Read Counts vs Cell Counts

In the simulation used for the subsequent analysis, the updates used in (
1.3
) are used to define what is assumed to be the true (pseudo)count of the numbers of cells of each strain in each droplet. (Fractional values of the pseudocounts can, for the sake of argument, be assumed to represent incomplete cell division.) We can make further changes so that the simulation keeps track of both (1) the actual number of cells
\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(t)
(until
t=t_{b}
) that would be predicted to be observed as a consequence of the updates (
1.3
) using a perfect experimental apparatus, as well as (2) the number of (16S V4 region) reads
\vec{\mathbf{R}}_{d}(t)
that are likely to be observed in practice.

Three modifications are used to model the number of reads for each strain which the researcher is likely to measure.

One modification accounts for the accumulation of relic genetic material (RNA/DNA) in the droplets, a second modification accounts for the fact that different strains are likely to have different copy numbers of the 16S rRNA gene, and a final modification accounts for the possibility of nonzero droplet merging error at the end of the experiment. Note that no attempts were made to directly account for stochasticities in the amount of marker introduced in each droplet (which is assumed to have a low signal-to-noise ratio
{\frac{\sigma}{\mu}}
due to the efforts of the experimenter), or for potential PCR amplification biases. However the modification employed to account for 16S rRNA copy number variations resembles (at least superficially) the modelling of PCR amplification bias proposed in previous work
[
MWC19
]
, and thus can likely without loss of generality be assumed to account for both.

Figure A.2.10
:
One strain outnumbers the other strain 2:1. Yet because the latter strain has a larger 16S gene copy number, the observed ratio of reads is 2:3.

For each time step, the change in the observed number of reads
R^{(s)}_{d}(t)
for each strain
s
in a given droplet
d
is assumed to follow from the true (pseudo)counts
N^{(s)}_{d}(t)
of the numbers of cells for each strain using the update steps:

\vec{\mathbf{R}}_{d}(t_{\beta\!+\!1})-\vec{\mathbf{R}}_{d}(t)=\left(\vec{\mathbf{B}}\ast\left(\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(t\!+\!\Delta t)-\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(t)\right)\right)_{+}\,,

(2.1)

where
\ast
denotes the entrywise or Hadamard product,
(x)_{+}
denotes the positive part of
x
, i.e.
\max\{x,0\}
, and
\vec{\mathbf{B}}
denotes the vector whose entries contain the 16S rRNA copy numbers (and/or PCR amplification biases) for each strain. The idea is to take the total number of cells of each strain which have ever been alive in the droplet and then multiply those numbers by the corresponding 16S copy numbers/PCR amplification biases. This is since the total number of currently living cells
n^{(s)}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}n^{(s)}(\tau)}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau
is equal to the total number of cells which have ever lived
\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}n^{(s)}(\tau)}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\right)_{+}\mathrm{d}\tau
minus the total number of cells which have ever died
\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}n^{(s)}(\tau)}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\right)_{-}\mathrm{d}\tau
,
(x)_{-}:=\max\{-x,0\}
. Compare with Figure
A.2.11
.

Figure A.2.11
:
The true number of cells observed at the end of the simulation is the green area minus the red area, whereas the number of reads actually observed at the end of the simulation is proportional to the green area only.
(NB: This is a heuristic plot illustrating the idea and not data from an actual simulation.
)

Thus the difference between the total number of cells which have ever lived and the total number of currently living cells, after being multiplied by the copy numbers and/or PCR amplification biases
\vec{\mathbf{B}}
, can be assumed to approximately correspond to the amount of relic genetic material (RNA/DNA) in the droplet. (At least assuming little to no cell lysis and/or nucleoside scavenging has occurred before the time
b
when the droplet’s contents are finally measured.) Thus vector
\vec{\mathbf{B}}
(entrywise) multiplied by the total number of cells which have ever lived can be assumed to correspond to the total number of reads observed from both living cells and from relic genetic material, thus (
2.1
) encompasses both of the first two aforementioned modifications.

Regarding the last of the three aforementioned modifications, at the end of the simulation, if a non-zero merging error rate
m
is assumed, then
\lfloor{D}\cdot(m/2)\rfloor
droplets are randomly selected without replacement to be merged into other droplets, and another
\lfloor{D}\cdot(m/2)\rfloor
droplets are randomly selected without replacement to be the droplets which are randomly merged into. Then for each of the
\lfloor{D}\cdot(m/2)\rfloor
pairs of droplets, a new droplet consisting of the sum of the observed contents from each droplet of the pair is created, with the values from the two older droplets discarded (as they are assumed to be unobserved). (The indices of which droplets were merged with which other droplets are recorded, and the original droplets for the cell pseudocounts are left unchanged.)

3
Modelling PCR Amplification

A (naively) simple model assumes that the PCR amplification factor for each kind
k
of genetic material (either the spikein gene or the 16S rRNA gene of one of the
S
strains) equals a constant
a
multiplied by a kind-specific instance of a log-normal random variable
\xi^{(k)}
. (This assumes that some sort of multiplicative central limit theorem is applicable.) Thus after amplification the (amplified) reads
\vec{\mathbf{A}}_{d}(t_{b})
corresponding to droplet
d
would equal

\vec{\mathbf{A}}_{d}(t_{b}):=\vec{\mathbf{R}}_{d}(t_{b})\ast(a_{d}\vec{\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})=\left(a_{d}\xi^{(1)}_{d}R^{(1)}_{d}(t_{b}),\dots,a_{d}\xi^{(s)}_{d}R^{(s)}_{d}(t_{b}),\dots,a_{d}\xi^{(S)}_{d}R^{(S)}_{d}(t_{b})\right)\,.

(3.1)

Letting
M
denote the copy number for the spikein gene (which recall is Poisson distributed, with a high expectation, cf. footnote
14
from section
4.3
), it follows that after normalizing by the observed number of spikein gene amplicons, the observed number of reads
\vec{\mathbf{O}}_{d}(t_{b})
corresponding to droplet
d
is

\vec{\mathbf{O}}_{d}(t_{b}):=\left(\frac{1}{a_{d}\xi^{M}_{d}M_{d}}\right)\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}_{d}(t_{b})=\left(\frac{\xi^{(1)}_{d}}{\xi^{M}_{d}M_{d}}R^{(1)}_{d}(t_{b}),\dots\frac{\xi^{(s)}_{d}}{\xi^{M}_{d}M_{d}}R^{(s)}_{d}(t_{b}),\dots,\frac{\xi^{(S)}_{d}}{\xi^{M}_{d}M_{d}}R^{(S)}_{d}(t_{b})\right)\,.

(3.2)

Then the hope is that
\mathbb{E}[M]\cdot\vec{\mathbf{O}}_{d}(t_{b})\approx\vec{\mathbf{N}}_{d}(t_{b})
, or at least
{\mathbb{E}[M]\cdot\vec{\mathbf{O}}_{d}(t_{b})\approx\vec{\mathbf{R}}_{d}(t_{b})}
, “on average” (i.e. the variance of the
\log(\xi_{d})
’s is not too large).

4
Example Cell and Read Count Trajectories

For all simulations, the carrying capacity
K
was set to
K=268
(based on what my collaborator said was realistic). Likewise for all simulations, the timestep
\Delta t
was set to
0.0001
, the “noise scale” was set to
\eta=8
, and the cells were assumed to be grown “in the fifth batch” with
200
timesteps separating each batch (so the simulation was ran for
1,000
time steps total). All simulations were initialized with one cell each of the two strains.

The copy numbers for both strains were always set to be
1
.

Appendix A.3
Parametric Estimation of Interaction Coefficients

The goal of this section is to modify the regression-based methodology used in
[
VCF+18
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
MBH+14
]

[
FM14
]

[
MMV+08
]
to not require longitudinal data, and to be applicable to pseudo-longitudinal data. (Unfortunately preliminary data suggests that the resulting methodology does not work well in practice.) Future work should compare the ideas herein with the ideas in
[
SSB+17
]
, which proposes modifying the aforementioned methodology to be applicable to a different
62
62

62

I.e. as opposed to the kind of cross-sectional data that pseudo-longitudinal data is.
kind of cross-sectional data. Cf. section
6.2
.

1
Details

Given any estimand which is a conditional expectation, i.e. of the form
\mathbb{E}[X\nonscript\;\nonscript\;A]
for some random variable
X
and some event
A
in the underlying sigma-algebra, in what follows the corresponding empirical (arithmetic) mean will be denoted

\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}[X\nonscript\;\nonscript\;A]:=\frac{\sum_{d\in[{D}]}X_{d}\mathbb{I}\!\left\{A\right\}}{\sum_{d\in[{D}]}\mathbb{I}\!\left\{A\right\}}\,.

(1.1)

In particular the unconditional empirical mean is

\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}[X]:=\frac{1}{{D}}\sum_{d\in[{D}]}X_{d}\,.

(1.2)

If one assumes the transition likelihoods are implicitly specified by the relationships:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\log(N^{(s)}(t_{\beta}))-\log(N^{(s)}(t_{\beta\!-\!1}))=r_{s}+\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}N^{(\sigma)}(t_{\beta\!-\!1})+\varepsilon&N^{(s)}(0)>0\\
N^{(s)}(t_{\beta})=0&N^{(s)}(0)=0\end{cases}\,,

((
7.15
) revisited)

for all
\beta\in[b]
and all
s\in[S]
(as discussed in further detail in section
7.5
), then as a result of the linearity of (conditional) expectation it follows that for any
b\in[B]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]

(1.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{s}+\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{(\sigma)}(t_{b\!-\!1})\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\,.

(1.4)

Thus one would then hope that for any
b\in[B]
as the number of observations increases

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]-\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]

(1.5)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle r_{s}+\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[N^{(\sigma)}(t_{b\!-\!1})\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\,.

(1.6)

On this basis, below, for all
s\in[S]
the least squares estimators

\displaystyle(\hat{r_{s}},\hat{\alpha_{1s}},\dots,\hat{\alpha_{Ss}}):=

\displaystyle\argmin_{(\tilde{r_{s}},\tilde{\alpha_{1s}},\dots,\tilde{\alpha_{Ss}})}\sum_{b=2}^{B}\left|\left|\vphantom{\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}}\left(\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\right.\right.\right.

(1.7)

\displaystyle\left.\phantom{\argmin_{(\tilde{r_{s}},\tilde{\alpha_{1s}},\dots,\tilde{\alpha_{Ss}})}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\right)

(1.8)

\displaystyle\left.\left.-\left(\tilde{r_{s}}+\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\tilde{\alpha_{\sigma s}}\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[N^{(\sigma)}(t_{b\!-\!1})\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\right)\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\,.

(1.9)

are considered.

Technically speaking, it is actually necessary to consider the set:

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{s}:=

\displaystyle\left\{b\in[B]\setminus\{1\}:\text{ the events }\{N^{(s)}(t_{b})>0\}\text{ and }\{N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1})>0\}\right.

(1.10)

\displaystyle\left.\vphantom{\left\{b\in[B]\setminus\{1\}:\text{ the events }\{N^{(s)}(t_{b})>0\}\right.}\phantom{b\in[B]\setminus\{1\}:}\text{ are both observed at least once}\right\}\,,

(1.11)

such that the computed estimators actually are

\displaystyle(\hat{r_{s}},\hat{\alpha_{1s}},\dots,\hat{\alpha_{Ss}}):=

\displaystyle\argmin_{(\tilde{r_{s}},\tilde{\alpha_{1s}},\dots,\tilde{\alpha_{Ss}})}\sum_{b\in\mathscr{B}_{s}}\left|\left|\vphantom{\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}}\left(\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\right.\right.\right.

(1.12)

\displaystyle\left.\phantom{\argmin_{(\tilde{r_{s}},\tilde{\alpha_{1s}},\dots,\tilde{\alpha_{Ss}})}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\right)

(1.13)

\displaystyle\left.\left.-\left(\tilde{r_{s}}+\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\tilde{\alpha_{\sigma s}}\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}\left[N^{(\sigma)}(t_{b\!-\!1})\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\right)\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\,.

(1.14)

When
\mathscr{B}_{s}=\emptyset
, the estimates are marked as missing. Just like for the estimands from chapter
7
, in analyses of simulation data, when estimates of the interaction coefficients are missing, they are conservatively set to
0
. (I.e. in the absence of any evidence for an interaction, the choice is made to prefer the possibility of making the fase negative error of assuming no interaction exists when one might exist, rather than possibly making the false positive error of assuming an interaction exists when in fact none exists.)

Note that starting from (
7.15
) and considering instead the relationship:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1})=n,N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]

(1.15)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1})=n,N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]

(1.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{s}+\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma=1}^{S}\alpha_{\sigma s}\mathbb{E}\left[N^{(\sigma)}(t_{b\!-\!1})\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1})=n,N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]\,,

(1.17)

following reasoning analogous to (
1.12
) would lead directly to the linear regression used in e.g.
[
VCF+18
]

[
MBH+14
]

[
FM14
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
MMV+08
]
to estimate interaction coefficients. However, estimating the quantities of the form:

\mathbb{E}\left[\log(N^{(s)}(t_{b}))\nonscript\;\middle\nonscript\;N^{(s)}(t_{b\!-\!1})=n,N^{(s)}(0)>0\right]

(1.18)

requires being able to observe the value of
N^{(s)}
at more than one time point for a single droplet, which is impossible for the data relevant to this study. As a reminder, this data is not actually longitudinal. Thus it seems one is limited to using (
1.12
), or something conceptually similar, when attempting to modify the approach from previous work
[
VCF+18
]

[
FM14
]

[
MBH+14
]

[
SBT+13
]

[
MMV+08
]
to be applicable to this data.

2
Comments

The only reason for including the parameters
r_{s}
in the model (
7.15
) used in this work is simply to ensure that “baseline” or “constant” growth effects are not misattributed as being part of the effects of the microbial interactions. Indeed, unlike the papers
[
VCF+18
]
,
[
MBH+14
]
, and
[
MMV+08
]
, the previous work
[
FM14
]
did not include (analogues of) the parameters
r_{s}
in their model, so there would be precedent for omitting them.

The assumption that the temporal dynamics can be adequately described by the equations (
7.15
) also implies at least two important assumptions:

1.

Because the equations (
7.15
) allow for indefinite population growth, using them implicitly assumes that all of our observations correspond only to the regime before resource scarcity and other factors begin to constrain the growth of microbial cells. Cf. the discussion from section
4.2
.

2.

The equations (
7.15
) implicitly assume that only pairwise interactions between different microbial species are relevant for determining their rates of growth, and that there is no non-negligible effect from higher-order interactions involving three or more distinct species. This is reflected in the absence of any terms that are a function of the abundances of more than two species. This has been recognized as a major limitation of these equations for accurately describing microbial interactions in previous work
[
MXS17
]
. Cf. also
[
BKK16
]
or the discussion from section
2
.

The second assumption was also reflected in the experimental design used in
[
VCF+18
]
, which nevertheless found the equations (
7.15
) adequate for describing the microbial interactions which they observed. In previous work the first assumption has been enforced via constraints on
\beta_{s}
and
\alpha_{s_{1}s_{2}}
which are not applied here, since such constraints seem to lack a biological justification, and they are unnecessary for the specific simulation model (
1.3
) that I used.

This quote from
[
MBH+14
]
is precedent for the above viewpoint on parameter constraints:

One approach to improving parameter estimation is to superimpose constraints on the parameters; however, unless these constraints can be justified both biologically and mathematically, they should not be enforced because the results can be greatly affected.
